us for the margin which we have put up with the New York Cotton Exchange

in regard to hedge transactions.

October 18, 1937
MEMORANDUM TO:
Mr. Joseph M. Jones
U. S. Department of State
Division of Far Fastern Ailairs
Washington, D. C.
FROM:
Orvis Brothers & Co.
60 Broadway
New York, N. Y.
SUBJECT:
The position of firms in Japan with New York
cotton futures contracts open on the books
of Orvis Brothers & Co.
Up to July of this year, cotton manufacturers and
importers in Japan made extensive use of the hedging facilities
of the New York Cotton Exchange. At a meeting in Japan early
this year between American and Japanese cotton textile interests
it was brought out that up to January 2oth Japanese cotton goods
manufacturers had booked oruers to supply 155 million square
yards of goods for delivery in the United States during 1937
against 75 million square yards in the whole of 1936. Their
forward sales to other countries must also have been large.
They did not have sufricient cotton, at the time when they sold
the goods, to meet their requirements for the manufacture of
those goods. Nor was the cotton available. It would become
vailable, however, with the movement of the American cotton
crop in August, and, in order to protect themselves against
possible loss should the price of the raw material rise prior
to the time of their being able to buy it, it was essential
for the Japanese cotton goods manufacturers to purchase cotton
futures contracts. As raw cotton became available, it would

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
be bought, and the futures contracts liquidated.
With regard to our Japanese cotton merchant clients,
they knew that ample cotton would be available when the 1937
American crop movement started, and, in selling raw cotton for
delivery later in the year, they also protected themselves
by buying futures contracts in New York.
The position, then, of our Japanese manufacturer
and merchant clients toward the end of July, 1937, when the
Japanese Exchange Control Commission began to hold up permits
to transfer Yen into Dollars, was that they were all owners
of New York cotton futures contracts which they had purchased
earlier in the year.
The New York Cotton Exchange has a rule permitting
its members to extend to their clients credit accomodation of
$5 a bale up to a maximum of 10,000. Orvis Brothers & Co.
extended credit to their Japanese friends in accordance with
this rule, and, until late last July, all calls for margin
that we made were responded to by the prompt cabled transfer
of the required amounts in Dollars. Toward the end of July,
the Japanese Exchange Control Commission began to hold up
requests for permission to transfer Yen into Dollars. Since
that time, the price of cotton has declined approximately $20
a bale, necessitating many margin calls. Most of these
calls were made through the intermediary of Orvis Brothers &
Company's correspondent in Osaka, Japan, who cabled us within
twenty four hours of each call to the effect that equivalent

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
amounts of Yen had been deposited with him by our clients
who had, at the same time, applied to the Japanese Exchange
Control Commission for permission to covert those Yen into
Dollars and remit them to us. To date, this permission has
not been granted.
The rules of the New York Cotton Lxchange make
it obligatory upon each member of the Exchange to demand full
payment by each customer of any amount owing at the close
of business of the day when the customer's account shows a
loss in excess of the credit extended to him. The rules
further require that payments in response to margin calls
must be made in Dollars.
Our friends in Japan are all substantial business
organizations, and when they accepted the credits which we
extended to them they agreed to respond to all calls that might
be necessitated by the cabled transfer of the required amounts
in Dollars. The action of the Japanese Exchange Control Com-
mission in holding up the requests of our friends to convert
Yen into Dollars has caused those friends to break their
agreements with us, and has been both a source of considerable
embarrassment to them and of great inconvenience to us.
Orvis Brothers & Co., and other members of the
New York Cotton Clearing Association, are required to deposit
with that Association each night the full amount of loss shown
on their clients' open contracts during the day. So long as
the Japanese Exchange Control Commission refuses to permit our

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Japanese clients to convert the Yen, which they have on deposit
on our behalf in Osaka, into Dollars, We are forced to make
these daily payments out of our own funds, the amount involved
now being so large as to be burdensome to us.
One of our largest clients in Japan is a leading
cotton manufacturing concern which exports the bulk of its
products. This concern bought 38o0 bales of New York cotton
futures between June 30th and July 2nd, 1937, at an average
price of about 11.90¢. It presumably sold goods on the basis
of that price, but can now buy its raw material 4¢ a pound
cheaper. This does not mean that it will have 2,000 per 100
bales more profit than it had originally counted on, as that
amount is shown as a loss on its hedging transaction. The
purchasing of the contracts virtually assured the concern that
it would neither profit nor lose by subsequent price fluctuations
in the raw material, and, although cotton is now much lower than
it was then the cost of its raw material will be what it pays for
it, plus the loss of its hedges. Presuming that the goods which
it sold forward on the basis of 1l.90¢ per pound cotton will be
exported, sufficient foreign exchange will come under the control
of the Japanese Government to cover the cost of the raw material
and the loss on the hedges, and it would appear to be only fair
that permission to convert enough Yen to offset the "futures" loss
should be granted.
The high regard which we have for our friends in
Japan, and our desire to do everything we possibly can to protect
their interests have given us no alternative but to continue to

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5-
carry the contracts which those friends have open on our books.
Anything the Department of State feels disposed to do to bring
to the Japanese Exchange Control Commission a realization of
the inequitable and unfortunate consequences of their prolonged
delay in permitting our friends to pay their Dollar obligations
to us will be highly appreciated.

and merohant olients toward the end of July, 1937, when
the Japanese Exchange Control Commission began to hold
up permits to transfer Yen into Dollars, was that they
were all owners of New York cotton futures contracts
which they had purchased earlier in the year.
The New York Cotton xchange has a rule permitting
its members to extend to their olients oredit accommoda-
t1on of 5 a bale up to a maximm of $10, 000. Orvi
Brothers & Co. extended oredit to their Japanese friends
In accordance with this rule, and, until late last July,
all calls for margin that we made were responded to by
the prompt cabled transfer of the required amounts in
Dollars. Toward the end of July, the Japanese Exchange
Control Commission began to hold up requests for permis-
sion to transfer Yen into Dollars. Since that time, the
priee of cotton has declined approximately 20 a bale,
necessitating many margin calls. Most of th ese calls
were made through the Intermediary of Orvis Brothers &
Company's correspondent in Osaka, Japan, who cabled us
within twenty four hours of eaoh call to the effect that
equivalent amounts of Yen had been deposited with him by
our olients who had, at the same time, applied to the
Jaranose

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
Japanese Exohange Control Commission for permission to
convert those Yen into Dollars and remit them to us. To
date, this permission has not been granted.
The rules of the New York Cotton Exohange make it
obligatory upon each member of the Exchange to demand full
payment by each customer of any amount owing at the close
of business of the day when the customer's account shows a
loss in excess of the oredit extended to him. The rules
further require that payments in response to margin calls
must be made in Dollars.
Our friends in Japan are all substantial business
organizations, and when they accepted the credits which
we extended to them they agreed to respond to all calls
that might be necessitated by the cabled transfer of
the required amounts in Dollars. The action of the
Japanese Exchange Control Commission in holding up the
requeats of our friends to convert Yen into Dollars has
caused those friends to break their agreements with us,
and has been both a source of considerable embarrassment
to them and of great inconvenienoe to us.
.Orvis Brothers & Co., and other members of the New
York Cotton Clearing Assoclation, are required to deposit
with that Association each night the full amount of loss
shown on their clients' open contracts during the day.
So long as the Japanese Exchange Control Commission re-
fuses to permit our Japanese clients to convert the Yen,
which they have on deposit on our behalf in Osaka, into
Dollars, we are forced to make these daily payments out
of our own funds, the amount involved now being so large
as to be burdensome to us.
One of our largest clients in Japan is a leading
cotton manufacturing concern whioh exports the bulk of
its products. This conoern bought 3800 bales of New
York cotton futures between June 30th and July 22nd,
1057, at an average price of about 1l.90g. It presuma-
bly sold goods on the basis of that price, but can now
buy its raw material 4g a pound oheaper. This does not
mean that 1t will have $2,000 per 100 bales more profit
than it had originally counted on, as that amount is
shown as a loss on its hedging transaction. The pur-
chasing of the contracts virtually assured the coneern
that it would neither prorit nor lose by subsequent prise
fluetuations in the raw material, and, although cotton is
now mueh lewer than it was then the sost of its raw
aterial will be what it pays for it, plus the loss of
em the basis of 1l.90d per pound cotton will be
ed. sufficient foreign exchange will come under
atrel of the Japanese Government to sover the

Subject: Blocked Funds
Mr. Orvis called in FE at the suggestion of Mr.
Feis to explain certain facts in connection with the
blooking in Japan of funds in yen belonging to him
894.5151/46
which have accrued there as a result of trading in
cotton futures for the accounts of several large
Japanese cotton spinning firms, and to request a
measure of assistance in securing foreign exchange for
these funds.
Mr. Orvis emphasized that he was not a mere ootton
speculator but that he was acting as an agent in the
kind of hedging whioh all cotton manufacturers are re-
F/A
quired to undertake against advance sales at a fixed
price of cotton textiles. The buying of cotton futures
is a kind of insurance for textile manufacturers and
is entirely legitimate.
The hedge contracts in question allow for a
fluctuation of twenty percent in the prioe of cotton,
beyond
11

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
beyond which margin must be supplied. The price of
cotton has declined sharply and Mr. Orvis has about
$200,000 in yen deposited in Japanese banks accruing
from margin payments. The contracts in question were
effected after the exchange restrictions of January 8,
1937, were placed in effect. However, Mr. Orvis states
that Mr. Sonoda, Vice President of the Yokohama Specie
Bank, said that the Japanese exchange control committee
realized the necessity for hedging transactions and
had promised that exchange would be granted to margin
such transactions provided they were not out of line
with the firm's usual business. Until six weeks ago
exchange was granted freely for funds of the type under
consideration but there has been no exchange granted
sinoe that time. The Japanese authorities have not re-
fused foreign exohange but they do not grant it. Mr.
Orvis has made no new commitments since foreign ex-
change ceased to be granted but margin payments on pre-
vious contracts continue to be deposited to his account
and he cannot get them released. Meanwhile he is
obliged by the New York Cotton Exchange to put up a
similar amount of money in dollars.
The contracts in question call for payment in
dollars; the fact that the Japanese Government does not
grant

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
grant exchange apparently does not affect the legal
obligation of the Japanese spinning firms concerned.
Failure to secure foreign exchange will cause the
cancellation of the remaining hedge contracts held
by Mr. Orvis and future harship for the spinning firms
in question.
Mr. Orvis was requested to put his case in writing
and submit it in the form of an informal memorandum,
and was informed that consideration would be given in
the Department to requesting American representatives in
Tokyo to inquire into the reasons for the delay in
granting foreign exchange. In response to a question,
Mr. Orvis was informed that a similar inquiry at the
Japanese Embassy in Washington concerning the causes
of the delay would not be out of order.

and at the present rate of consuiption will be entirely

shipments would be safe and that payment would be assured

that settlement coula be made. Such certificates are
a prerequisite to an application for foreign exchange,
but are no guarantee whatever that foreign exchange
will be granted.

September 22, 1937.
JAPAN'S ULTIMATE FOREIGN EXCHANGE RESOURCES
The ability of Japan to carry on a protracted war
depends ultimately upon her actual and potential foreign
894.515
exchange resources. Warfare requires many vital raw
materials which Japan does not possess at all or in
sufficient quantities, and which must be purchased with
foreign exchange. It is important, therefore, to
1/
attempt an estimate of the foreign exchange which Japan
52
can muster. This estimate must necessarily be made on
the assumption that Japan will impose whatever internal
1
control measures are found necessary to make use of
2
potential resources.
Gold and Silver Reserves
Until recently Japan's gold reserve has been car-
ried on the books of the Bank of Japan at its pre-
devaluation exchange value. This reserve has recently
been revalued at the present world market value less
10 percent allowance for price fluctuation. At the
F/WB
revalued price, Japan's goh reserve on August 25 was
about 1,154,000,000 yen. At the current rate of exchange
this

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
this would be worth about 1,269,400,000 yen. By Auguct 25
gold to the value of 476,788,000 yen (present exchange value)
had already been shipped to the United States, and since
that time 122,062,000 yen more has been shipped, leaving the
gold holdings of the Bank of Japan at 1,147,338,000 yen
(present exchange value). It is reported that the reserves
of the Bank of Japan will be maintained at about 800,000,000
yen, the new note issue will be maintained at about
1,800,000,000 yen, and 347,338,000 yen of surplus go1d wi11 thus
be readily available for export. In case of urgent war needs,
economic controls will be established which will permit prac-
tically all of Japan's gold reserve to be used for shipment
abroad. Actually, however, this would probably not be done
except in the case of dire necessity. The Japanese law govern-
ing the Bank of Japan provides for a fiduciary note issue of
1,000,000,000 yen against Government bonds and Treasury
certificates and other gilt-edge securities or commercial
bills, while all notes issued above that amount must be
fully covered by gold, any excess being subject to a tax
of not less than 3 percent per annum. Thus in recent years
the gold reserve of the Bank of Japan was carried at about
500,000,000 yen, and the total note issue fluctuated around
the maximum permitted, that is, 1,500,000,000 yen. The
increase in note issue in June and July of 1937 above the
maximum

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
maximum was one of the principal factors forcing the reval-
uation of the gold reserve to a figure more in line with
the exchange value of the yen and the increase in the book
value of the reserves of the Bank.
The Bank of Japan reported on June 19 a reserve of
coin and bullion other than gold valued at 55,559,000 yen.
The reserves of gold, silver and foreign exchange
maintained by the Banks of Taiwan and Cnosen are not
known. The note issues of these two banks combined was
about 225,000,000 yen at the end of June. It is known
that these issues are inadequately covered by gold and
silver reserves but that they are in large part supported
by Bank of Japan notes. The reserves, therefore, probably
do not exceed 100,000,000 yen. It is doubtful that these
reserves would be used except in extremity.
Gold Production
There has been a steady increase in recent years in
the gold production of the Japanese Empire, from 47,000,000
yen in 1932 to 145,000,000 yen in 1936. It may be that
the increased purchase price fixed by the Bank of Japan
early this year will result in increased production. An
eetimate of 200,000,000 yen annual gold production in the
future is a liberal one.
Reserves

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.98)
-4一
Reserves of Gold and Foreign Exchange Held Abroad
In previous years the Yokohama Specie Bank has
attempted to maintain its foreign exchange reserves abroad
at between 300 and 400 million yen. It was generally be-
lieved that by the end of 1936 these reserves had been
largely depleted, this being an important reason for the
weakness of the yen at that time, the strengthening of
exchange control, and for the beginning of gold shipments
in March. Although 598,000,000 yen of gold has thus far
either shipped or engaged for shipment from Japan since
March, information secured by the Treasury indicates that
no surplus reserves have been built up in this country; in
fact, the Yokohama Specie Bank at present has an overdraft
in New York for $7,400,000, whereas other Japanese banks
have credits of $6,400,000 leaving a net overdraft of
$1,000,000; and it seems likely that if there is an over-
draft in New York, reserves in London must be small or
negligible. Most of the gold shipped to the United States was
immediately sod for sterling exchange which, according to
indications received by the Treasury, has not been used to
build up reserve balances but has been used in the settlement
of current trade accounts and for the retirement of old com-
mercial paper which is no longer welcome in the London market.
Foreign branches of Japanese enterprises probably
maintain liquid bank balances, but this is probably not
large

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
large enough to consider in a study of this nature.
Foreign Securities owned by Japanese Citizens
The April issue of the "Oriental Economist" published
figures concerning Japanese ownership of foreign securities
as of September 30, 1936; these figures were issued by the
Ministry of Finance which apparently requires the registra-
tion of all foreign securities. According to this source,
there were owned both at nome and abroad by Japanese citizens
on September 30, 1936, foreign securities valued t about
400,000,000 yen figures at the olá par of exchange (2 yen
$1.00) and 700,000,000 yen at the present exchange value
(3.5 yen = $1.00). Over two-thirds of these securities were
foreign government bonds and most of the remainder was in
shares in foreign concerns. (No Manchukuo Government bonds,
nor any shares or bonds in yen currency placed in Japan were
included in these figures). The figures given represent
the face values of the securities; it is probable that if
these securities were liquidated they would yield less than
face value in foreign exchange, but it seems likely that
about
500,000,000 yen could be realized.
Other Foreien Investments of Japan
Japanese public and private investments abroad are gen-
erally estimated at between 4 and 5 billions of yen. This
figure undoubtedly overlaps the figures given in the pre-
ceding

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.98)
一6 -
ceding section for Japanese ownership of foreign securi-
ties, and the difference between the two is probably rep-
resented by direct investments in China and Manchuria
(90 percent of all Japanese investments abroad are roughly
estimated to be in China and Manchuria) and in other coun-
tries. These investaents are extremely immobile, and for
the present purposes may be disregarded.
Foreign Trade
Foreign trade is a relative factor in Japan's foreign
exchange resources. During the first ei ht months of
1937 Japan has imported far more than she has exported,
and her debit trade balance for this period reached
774,231,000 yen, as compared with a debit of 264,670,000
yen for the same period of 1936; this import excess has
been made possible largely through the sale of other erchange
resources (in this case, gold).
Before extended war operations in China occurred, the
"Oriental Economist" estimated in July that Japan's trade
balance for the year would show a debit of not more than
350,000,000 yen. This was based upon the facts that, (a)
Japan

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Japan normally incurs trade deficits in the first half of
the year which are compensated by heavy exports in the
last half of the year, which is the heavy export season;
(b) stocks of cotton are one-third higher than normal, and
stocks of other raw materials are far above average; and
(c) exchange control will be used to reduce non-essential
imports.
The development of the Chinese conflict into an ex-
tended war seems to necessitate considerable revision in
the foregoing estimate. Imports larger than would other-
wise be necessary undoubtedly will be stimulated, whereas
exchange control, and war needs and operations,will probably
interfere with the export trade. It seems quite unlikely
that Japan would allow any material depletion at this time
of her raw materials stocks while the outcome of the war is
in doubt; fear of even higher prices, of sanctions, of the
United States neutrality law, et cetera, will probably cause
her to continue substantial purchases of raw materials.
As the war continues, as prices rise, as exchange control
diverts raw materials from export industries to war indus-
tries, as shipping is diverted to war needs, the declines
in export trade may be expected to be progressively more
rapid. It is probable that Japan'e import surplus in 1937
will reach nearly 1 billion yen. Japan's foreign trade for
the

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
the month of August was a debit of 53,000,000 yen, whereas
in August of 1936 there was a credit balance of about
35,000,000 yen.
Other Items in the Balance of Payments
Naturally other items than trade in the balance of
payments affect Japan's foreign exchange position. Factors
such as shipping, insurance, interest, and so forth usually
yield credits to Japan's balance of paymentε of from 150 to
200 million yen. This credit may be expected to decline.
Japan's shortage of shipping facilities in the last few
months is well known. Diversion of Japanese shipping to
war purposes, and increased payments to foreign ships should
reduce the above-mentioned lump credit materially.
Loans
The excellence of Japan's credit standing makes it
impossible to rule out the possibility of foreign loans.
Probably the simplest expedient for securing foreign funds
would be the re-sale abroad of Japanese foreign currency
bonds owned by Japanese citizens. Outstanding Japanese
foreign currency bonds amount to about 2,500,000,000 yen
(present exchange value), of which about 40 percent (1 bill-
ion yen) are owned by Japanese citizens at home and abroad.
These securities are now quoted at fair prices (75 to 85)
on the exchanges; clearly, unlading of these securities
by

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.96)
-9-
by their owners upon request of the Government would drive
down their prices, but substantial quantities of foreign
exchange could be realized. The possible success of an
issue of new foreign currency bonds by Japan is a conjec-
tural matter.

These resources are those which Japan can herself supply,
and takes no account of foreign lans, resale of re-
patriated foreign currency bonds, etc. About 40 percent
of Japan's foreign currency bonds, which amount to
about 2,500,000,000 yen, are owned by Japanese citizens,
and could possibly be resold abroad,but probably at a
large discount.

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
Conclusions
1. Japan's theoretical foreign exchange resources
total about 6 billions of yen (not counting trade, loans,
et cetera) but her liquid or convertible resources of for-
eign exchange are only about 2 billions of yen.
2. Two billions of yen are probably sufficient to
finance the deficits in Japan's foreign trade, even con-
sidering war necessities, for at least one year, without
difficulty, and
ythrough a second year, although
Japan's operations during this second year would grow
cumulatively more difficult, and at its end Japan's foreign
exchange resources would be completely exhausted.
3. Foreign loans in any form would of course prolong
Japan's enduring capacity; the possibility of substantial
exchange from these sources is at least worth considering.

For several months this company has been
endeavoring to secure payment of a dividend declared
894.5151757
by the Ford Motor Company of Japan Limited, Yokohama,

are covered fully in the attached memorandum. You
will note that the stock of the Ford Motor Company of
Japan Limited is owned completely by the Ford Motor
Company, a Delaware Corporation, and five individuals,
all of whom are American citizens.
In view of the unusual conditions existing
in Japan at the present time the Manager of the Ford
Motor Company of Japan has endeavored to arrange
remittance of this sum exclusively through the Jap-
anese Ministry of Finance and has not appealed to the
American Embassy at Tokyo for assistance, nor do we
consider it advisable for him to do so. It would be
appreciated, however, if the State Department would
request the Embassy to assist, if possible, in secur-

Following up their previous visit of November 8, 1937 (see

letter formally requesting the State Department to assist, through

F/mR
fer into dollars of funds amounting to $887,088.46. These funds
represent the dividend declared upon the shares of the Ford Motor
Company of Japan, Ltd., which are owned almost entirely by the
Ford Motor Company in this country.

Japanese

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)

Japanese authorities the deley in the granting of such permits

Embassy. For some reason which was not given, the Ford Motor

the Embassy on the question; neither was it willing to pursue

funds in payment for materials shipped by the Ford Motor Company
to its branch in Japan during the first six months of 1937.
Mr. Jones accepted the letter submitted and said that the
matter would be given proper consideration.

Mr. Haas
Subject: Japan's Foreign Exchange Resources.
Summary
FW
Conclusion:
08
Japan can continue military operations upon the
6
present scale for another year before exhausting her
4
foreign exchange resources.
5
However, the possibility of war with Russia makes
it highly desirable for Japan to conserve for that con-
5
tingency as much of her exchange resources as possible.
Notwithstanding that contingency the amount of
foreign exchange resources that Japan will use up dur-
C
ing the next three months does not appear to be enough
to force a settlement of the Sino-Japanese war. The
decisive factors in her Chinese campaign during the
next few months seem to us to be political and mili-
tary - not shortage of foreign exchange.
I. How much foreign exchange resources has Japan now?
(a) She can with ease obtain $200 million.
(b) If pressed to the limit, she could double
that sum without increasing her exports or decreasing
her imports.

value of $600 to $700 m1llion.
II. How fast is Japan using up her exchange resources?
Apparently at the rate of $30 to $40 million a
month.
At that rate Japan can go at least five months
before using up her "ready" foreign exchange resources;
and at least twelve months before exhausting all her
foreign exchange resources.
Moreover, if forced by military need, there are
numerous measures that Japan can resort to in order to
increase her foreign exchange resources. These meas-
ures, if adopted, could greatly extend the period in
which Japan could carry on military operations.
TII. What will be her foreign exchange situation if
U.S.S.R. enters the fight?
Japan will need much more war material than she is
now using up. How muoh more we do not know, but a con-
servative estimate would seem to be an increase in con-
sumption of oil, machinery, war material of double cur-
rent uses. (Our Army and Navy Departments can probably
estimate Japan's monthly needs of imported war material
in a war with U.S.S.R. and China.)
Faced with the danger of disappearing as a major
power, Japan would leave no step untried to strengthen
her weakest economic link -- her need for foreign
exohange. Confronted with that possibility Japan could
probably

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.96)
3
probably develop enough of a surplus of foreign exchange
to meet the needs of a large sealc war for six months or
SC.
By then her assets convertible into foreign exchange
will be exhausted and her position will grow precarious.
Italy and Germany can and will help, but their own needs
will leave little for Japan.
The decisive factor in a war with U.S.S.R. and China
lasting more than six months will be the aid other coun-
tries will give Japan - either directly or by giving aid
to Germany and Italy. That aid can take the form of
either increased purchases of Japanese goods or of export
credits. Loans to Japan will be a very poor financial
risk - but political considerations, not financial, will
doubtless be the determining factor.

JAPAN'S FOREIGN EXCHANGE RESERVES
1. How much foreign exchange resources does Japan
now have?
2. At what rate is Japan losing her foreign ex-
change resources?
3. What will be her position with respect to for-
eign exchange should war with the Soviet Union break out
within the next six months?
1. How much foreign exchange resources does Japan now
have?
We estimate the total of the assets which can pro-
vide foreign exchange at once to be at least $475 million,
and probably 600 to 700 million.
Her foreign exchange resources consist of:
(a) gcld in the Bank of Japan; in a special secret
account; and in private hoards.
(b) foreign balances of banks and nationals of
Japan.
(c) foreign securities owned by Japanese.
(a) Japanese securities originally issued in foreign
ourrencies now owned by Japanese, which can be resold
abroad.
(e) recoverable gold and silver.
These resources can be converted into foreign ex-
change at any time, and are to be distinguished from
foreign exchange assets which become currently available.
The estimate indicated above is arrived at as follows:
(a) Gold.
On the basis of the information available, we
estimate that Japan had from $270 to $290 million of
gold on November 30, 1937, as follows:

During November 1937, the Bank of Japan reported
its gold holdings at approximately $267 million. Any
gold which Japan has in excess of that amount is kept

from the revaluation of gola holdings of August i, 1937,
and could be sold abroad without public record. Áll the
gold which has been exported since the summer of 1937
has come out of this secret fund. How much is left we
do not know but on the basis of the above estimates of
the gold holdings, there is probably as much gold left
as was taken over from the Banks of Taiwan and Chosen.
As noted above, that may be as much as $40 millions,
though none wae included in our estimate of the gold
holdings available.
Of the $267 million in possession of the Bank of
Japan $200 million is necessary to satisfy the existing
legal reserve requirements of the banking system. That
leaves $67 million of "free" gold plus whatever suus are
in the secret fund which could be sold in foreign mar
kets without new legislation.
(b) Foreign Exchange Balances.
Japan had on November 30, $50 million balances in
this country. The foreign exchange balances which Japan
holds in foreign markets outside of the United States
are not available to us. They can only be estimated.
Japan normally transacts a substantial portion of her
ourrent trade with short-term sterling credits. Usually
her sterling balances are higher than her dollar balances,
but in recent months Japan has been hard pressed for
exchange and it is not at all unlikely that Japan's
sterling balances were down to a very low level.
In recent months there has been a reluctance in
England to accept Japanese paper, with the result that
Japan has had to conduct a larger proportion of her
1mport

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.98)
17
import trade on a cash basls. This has meant that a
larger portion of her available exchange has had to be
used for payment of goods than would otherwise have
been the case. In the past few weeks the situation
has been improved so that it is quite possible that
she has buiit up her sterling balances to 2 to 4 mil-
lion pounds. In view of the usual foreign balances
necessary to facilitate Japan's trade, total sterling
balanoes of 2 to 4 million pounds may be much too low
an estimate, yet a higher one is not warranted on the
basis of the information we have.
(c) Japanese owned foreign securities.
Japanese nationals and institutions possess for-
eign securities which they can sell abroad. In the
Oriental Economist for October l937 an editorial com-
ment gives the amount as being in excess of 1 billion
yen, or about $325 million. This figure, however,
comes from a biased source and was offered apparently
for the purpose of proving that Japan has adequate for
eign exchange resources to meet any emergency. Unfor
tunately it 1s the only figure available to us. It is
very likely too high. On the basis of the reported
amount of interest income from foreign securities, which
is included in the annual study of the balance of pay-
ments of Japan for 1935 we would estimate that Japan
had only half that amount, i.e., 500 million yen of
foreign securitien, or about $175 million.
The liquidation value of these securities can be
conservatively estimated at half their value. Some
portion of those seourities doubtless consist of
Chinese holdings, the marketable value of which, under
present conaitions, is almost nil. (This figure, it
must be remembered, does not include direct investments
in China and Manchuria.) However, the bulk of it con-
sists of securities of countries other than China and
Manchuria. It is, therefore, conservative to estimate
that Japan can realize $85 million on the foreign hold-
ings of her nationals.
(a) Japanese foreign currency securities.
Japan has been re-buying Japanese obligations which
had been 1ssued in foreign countries prior to 1929. The
Oriental Economist says that Japanese nationals and
institutions had acquired 76l million yen, par value of
these

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
these securities. This figure may be fairly accurate.
Japan may acquire foreign exchange by selling surrep-
titiously, if necessary, these securities in New York,
London, Paris and Berlin, where Japanese securities
now in the possession of foreigners are currently
bought and sold. However, Japan would not be able to
dispose of a large volume of these securities without
breaking the price which is now much below par. We
estimate that Japan might be able to acquire at least
$25 million of foreign exchange by marketing the securi-
ties gradually.
(e) Recoverable gold and silver.
Japan may make an attempt to recover gold and silver
now in jewelry, ornaments, etc. It 1s impossible to
estimate the amount of foreign exchange assets which
these desperate measures may give to Japen but it is
11kely to be at least $25 m1llion.
The total foreign exchange resources of Japan con-
sisting of gold, foreign balances, marketable foreign
securities, sale of Japanese securities, amounts of
$475 million. As noted above these estimates are very
conservative. It is not at all unlikely that the
actual amounts exceed these estimates by $1o0 million
or more.
2.
At what rate is Japan losing her foreign exchange
resources?
We estimate during recent months that Japan has
been paying out on balance from 30 to 40 million dollars
a month. On her trade balance Japan during the past
year lost about 630 million yen of foreign exchange,
while her balance of payments on items other than trade
have yielded a small excess. During next year we esti-
mate the adverse balance will be from 50 to 60 million
dollars a month.

For 1938 the balance of trade 1s estimated to be
about a billion yen. The estimate is based upon the
following assumptions:
(a) The imports of war materials will continue at
a higher level than during the latter part of 1937
to make up for the depleted stock stored up before
hostilities began.
(b) The imports of raw materials needed for the
manufacture of export commodities, such as cotton
and wool, must be revised in 1938. During the
second half of 1937 imports of these commodities
were greatly reduced. Reliance was had upon the
ex1sting stocks which had been built up in antici-

stocks, however, particularly cotton and wool,
will be almost exhausted by the early part of 1938.
The rate of consumption of Important raw materials,
therefore -- under the assumption of continuing
hostilities - will be during 1938 greater than in
1937.
(c) The restrictions whioh the Japanese Government
has placed upon imports of non-essential commodities

(d) Japan is able to retain her export markets.
It is assumed that the effect of more intensive

The basis of our estimates of a net adverse balance
of 100 million yen for 1938 1s as follows:
(a) Net shipping and insurance income.
A part of the Japanese fleet has been removed from
international trade to carry men, munitions and supplies
to the Asiatic mainland. It seems likely that the
Japanese receipts from the shipping trade, including
insurance, will no more than balance income, while such
a large part of her mercantile marine is engaged in war
traffic.
(b) Income from foreign investments
In 1935, Japan received income from foreign securi-
ties of only 25 million yen. This income will be un-
affected by the war, unless Japan is impelled to sell
her foreign holdings. The balance of the income from
this 1tem (25 million yen) is income from Japanese enter
prises abroad. Most of the enterprises were in China.
This source of income was reduced severely in the latter
part of 1937, and will continue at the low level in 1938
if the present hostilitles continue.
(c) Tourist and diplomatic expenditures
The tourist traffic in Japan has probably been
reduced considerably, if not cut off entirely, and will
remain

remain so as long as there is danger in travel to the
Far East. Diplomatic expenditures in Japan change
little, so the figure of 50 million yen as compared with
115 for 1936 1s conservative.
(a) Immigrant Remittances
The Japanese emigrants will probably increase their
remittances to Japan because (a) their relatives living
in Japan will be in greater need owing to the increasing
cost of living, and (b) the Japanese government will
doubtless make strong patrictic appeals to Japanese liv-
ing abroad to purchase bonds of the Japanese government
in order to help win the war.
(e) Interest payments in forelgn-held Japanese
obligations
As long as Japan does not default in its obligations,
there is no reason why the amount of this expenditure
should decrease. If Japan has difficulties in obtaining
foreign exchange, however, she can default, or pay in
"blockcd" yen balances and thereby save about $60 mil-
lion per year of foreign exchange.
(f) Japanese expendilures abroad
Japan is likely to increase the expenditures abroad
on propaganda account in order to create a "favorable
1mpression" abroad.
(g) Net Government payments abroad.
We are not certain what this item represents. It
may repregent the payments to Russia for the Chinese
Eastern Railroad. In this case, the payments will
probably continue unless Japan gets into extreme diffi-
culties with her foreign exchange resources or U.S.S.R.
joins the struggle.
(c) Capital movements to and from Japan will probably

Most of Japan's direct investments abroad have
hitherto been in China and Manchuria. It is likely
that expenditures in those areas on public works of a
military nature will greatly increase during the next
year. However, whereas in earlier years expenditures
in China represented foreign exchange expenditures, the
expenditures which Japan will make during the next year,
although offset, of course, by merchandise transactions
technically in a foreign land, will actuelly be made in
yen. Hence, so far as her balance of payments is con-
cerned, most of her investments or expenditures on
capital account that will be made in China and Manchuria
should not be recorded.
Japan has an average of over a half million men in
Manchuria and China. They spend a portion of their pay
there, and large quantities of local supplies are pur
chased by the army. These transactions, however are
doubtless settled in yen and so do not add to Japan's
exchange burden.
Altogether

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
-14
Altogether examination of the various items enter
ing into her balance of payments leads to the conclusion
that the adverse balance Japan had to meet during the
past ten months was about 200 million dollars. This fig-
ure roughly checks with our estimate of her export of
gold and silver, after allowing for some reduction in her
foreign balances.
The rate of out-payments were less during the past
few months than during the earlier ones, owing to the
tightened restrictions on imports after September. For
1938, however, should hostilitles continue, her unfavor
able trade balance 1s likely to increase to 2 billion
yen, and her out-payments on account of invisible items
to 10o million. That means Japan will need about 55
million dollars a month of foreign exchange to meet her
payments.
Japan can obtain, in our opinion, enough foreign
exchange reserves now for a year's drain at that rate.
Moreover, she can, if necessary, reduce that net out-
payment without curtailing her military activities so
that her current exchange resources can go further than
a year.
3. Japan can, if pressed to the limit, substantially
increase her current supply of foreign exchange for
some time to come.
a. She can default on her foreign currency obli-
gations and block the balances due foreigners.
b. She can denude Japan, Korea and North China
of gold and silver ornaments and jevelry.
c. She can increase the rate f gold production
even though at a considerable cost in yen.
d. She can expand barter arrangements between
Germany, Italy and Manchuria and North China, swapping
Manchurian and North China produce for German and
Italian war material and thereby cutting down the
amount of purchases requiring foreign exchange.
e. She can obtain some credits from Germany and
Italy for purohase of war materials from those countries.
f. She

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
-15
f. She can keep her own imports down to the bone;
reduce North China'g imports from countries other than
Japan or Manchuria; and by depreciating the yen and yuan
exchange, and adopting other devices to increase foreign
consumption of her goods, Japan can increase exports
from areas under her control.
How much of an increase of foreign exchange Japan
can raise by adopting these measures cannot be known.
Yet it is certain to be substantial. It is not unlikely
that Japan can, if her national existence is at stake,
make available an additional 10 to 30 million dollars
a month. This additional sum of foreign exchange taken
togother with her present foreign exchange assets will
be much more than adequate for the Chinese campaign.
4. If Russia enters the conflict Japan's foreign ex-
change situation will become very serious.
In the first place Japan will need much more
material than called for in the present war.
Secondly, Japan's foreign trade will be less free.
Though U.S.S.R. navy is small, it probably can do some
damage to Japan's commercial fleet, or at least cause
insurance rates to increase, and increase oil consump-
tion of the Japanese fleet, etc.
Thirdly, unofficial boycotts against Japanese
goods will increase under the impact of growing world
war fears.
Fourthly, Japan will not be able to utilize Man-
churianand Chinese sources of supply to the same extent
as now, owing to the increased military activity in
those areas bound to take place in a major campaign.
Destruction of Japanese sources of supply through
Russian sympathizers and military, air and submarine
forces and guerilla bands 1s certain to be large.
Lastly, Japan's allies - Germany and Italy - would
not be in a position to help Japan in the way of needed
raw materials on credit, even if they do not immediately
become involved. With U.S.S.R. in the fight they will
be reluctant to part with much material because of the
possibility of having to be fully prepared for war them-
selves.
For

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
-16-
For Japan, the decisive factor in a struggle with
U.S.S.R. at this time might well be the financlal assis-
tance she may or may not get from foreign powere. Were
Japan unable to obtain substantial credits from some
foreign power and should unofficial boycotts of Japan-
ese products grow, Japan would be in a very precarious
position with respect to her ability to obtain foreign
materials necessary to continue a major campaign for
more than six months or so.

COMMENTS ON TREASURY DEPARTMENT'S GONFIDENTIAL
MEMORANDUM ON UAPAN S FOREIGN EXCHANGE
RESOURCES" (DATED DECEMBER 13, 1937)
I.
Gold Resources.
Treasury Department memorandum estimates that Japan
894.5151/62
had from $270 to $290 million in gold, as of November
30, 1937. Of this $267 million are credited to Bank of
Japan, and the undetermined balance to the Government's
secret gold fund. It is stated that $2o0 million in
gold are "necessary to satisfy the exlsting legal
reserve requirements of the banking system", leaving
$67 million available as "free gold plus whatever sums
are in the secret fund which could be sold in foreign
markets without new legislation."
On the basis of confidential figures and informa-
F/MR
tion furnished by the Research and Statistics Division
of the Federal Reserve Board, it has been possible to
arrive at a more definite estimate of the amount of gold
romaining in the Treasury's "secret" gold fund. Even
taking into account gold shipments subsequent to Novem-
ber 30, it appears that total Japanese gold holdings at
the

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
the present time are not far short of $300 million.
The basis on which this estimate is arrived at, with
comparisons with the Treasury's figures, is indicated
below.
1. The Treasury Department's estimate is derived
by taking the United States dollar equivalent of the
reported gold holdings of the Bank of Japan on March 1,
1937, deducting recorded gold exports from March through
November, and adding estimated new gold production in
Japanese-controlleā areao through the same period, plus
probable gold assets of the Banks of Chosen and Taiwan.
Ac a check against this estimate, the following computa-
tion takes as its starting point reported Japanese gold
holdings at the effective date of yen devaluation, Aug-
ust 25, 1937, since whioh Japan's gold assets have been
divided between the fixed gold reserves of the Bank of
Japan and the fluctuating holdings of the Treasury's
Special Gold Account, to which all new gold purchases
have been added, and from which all subsequent gold ship-
ments have been made.
2. Under the yen devaluation law of August 10, 1937,
the fine gold content of the yen was reduced from 750 to

7. In view of the slender holdings thus indicated

The Treasury Department memorandum states that 200 mil-
lion dollars (6l4 million yen) are required to satisfy
legal reserve requirements, leaving $67 million avail-

C

7
the Bank's note circulation stood at 1,786,644 yen,

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.97)

to expand further under continued Government deficit
(a)
financing, there does not appear to be warrant for re-

existing legal requirements, as being definitely avail-
able as "free" gola. This is, however, largely a matter
of technical distinction. If it became necessary to draw
upon the Bank's gold holainge, the Bank could under its
statutes partially substitute silver in its specie re-
or the fiduciary limit could itself be raised by special
legislation. The point is that the Bank's gold holdings,
so far maintained intact at $26l millions, constitute
Japan's last line of gold resources, which, within very
narrow limits, can be drawn on only by changing its
present currency law.

9. The Manchurian Gold Mining Company, formed in
1934, with capital subscribed jointly by the Manohukuo
Government and the Southern Manchurian Railway, has pur
sued a program of active exploitation of the gold re-

planned an output of 200 million yen for the five-year
period 1937-41. This envisages a virtual tripling of
the current rate of production.
In 1936 the Bank of Manchou purchased 13 million
yen of newly mined Manchurian gold. The Oriental Econ-

or about $4 1/2 million. There does not appear to be
evidence that Manchou gold has been imported into Japan.
Nevertheless, the gold holdings of Bank of Manchou (which
are not reported) and new production could be utilised in
financing military imports into Manchukuo.

II. Foreign Exchange Balances.
In the estimate of Japan's foreign exchange resources
compiled by J.M.Jones in mid-September, foreign exchange
balances held abroad by Japanese banks were set down as
"negligible". The Treasury Department's memorandum puts
Japanese bank balances in the United States at the end
of November at a figure of $50 million, with possible
sterling balances of 2 to 4 million pounds in London.
Jones' report stated that according to information
secured from the Treasury (thrcugh Mr. Lochhead):
"The Yokohama Specie Bank has at present (ca.
September 15) an overdraft in New York of $7,400,000,
whereas other Japanese banks have credits of
$6,400,000, leaving a net overdraft of $1,000,000;
and it seems likely that if there is an overdraft
in New York, reserves in London must be small or
negligible.n
The most recent report of the Treasury Department's
check-up on Japanese bank balances in New York reveals
that on December 8 these amounted to $45.2 million. A
check made two weeks earlier (November 24) disclosed
New York balances of $45 million, of which approximately
four fifths was held by the New York branch of the Yoko-
hama Specle Bank. The figure of $50 million for Japanese
bank

The most elusive item in the computation of Japan's
foreign exohange resources is the extent and valuation
of Japanese holdings of negotiable foreign currency
securities. All estimates emanating from Japanese
sources appear to be considerably in excess of those
indicated by capitalising reported income from such
investments, or by checks made by the Treasury Depart-
ment of Japanese holdings of dollar securities.

holdings of foreign securities by Japanese nationals

were
1/ Or1ental Economist, Apr11 1937, pp. 217-18.

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.98)
-18
were selling at par in New York in the early part of
1937 have been quoted during the year as low as 67 1/8,
although recent quotations have ranged between 75 and
80. Japanese Government 5 1/2's declined during 1937
from a high of 89 3/8 to a low of 52 1/2, with recent
quotations around 70. These declines have occurred
despite maintenance in full to date of debt service on
Japan's external loans. The increasing strain upon
Japan's foreign exchange resources is likely to make
the continuance of such service a matter of serious
difficulty, and to be reflected in a further deprecia-
tion in the liquidating value of such Japanese-held
obligations.
In the Treasury Department's memorandum it is esti-
mated that "Japan might be able to acquire at least $25
million of foreign exchange by marketing gradually" the
holdings of her nationals in the form of Japanese foreign
ourrency securities. Jones has made no allowance under
this heading. We are inclined to regard the Treasury
Department's estimate as a rather liberal one from the
standpoint of Japanese availabilities.
In the Russo-Japanese War Japan was able to finance
almost two thirds of the total cost of 1,720 million yen
through foreign loans. In the present conflict with
ah1na

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
China, in which Japan has been declared to have v1olated
the Nine Power Pacific Pact by all the signatorles except
Italy, she cannot count on floating loans in the countries
which could alone furnish them and where such credits are
most needed. Germany and Italy might be willing to sup
ply her with munitions on oredit, in return for certain
concessions in China, but they are unable to furnish the
raw materials for which alone Japan is externally depen-
dent.
v. Comparative Summary.
In the appended table there are brought together,
for purposes of comparison, the estimates of Japan's
foreign exchange resources contained in the memoranda
prepared (I) by J. M. Jones (September 20, 1937), (II)
by the Treasury Department (November 30), and (III) those
indicated by checks made by the writer as noted in the
foregoing analysis (December 15).
Jones' estimates were computed in yen and have been
converted into dollars at ourrent exchange rates for pur
poses of comparison with the other estimates expressed
in dollars. His total figure is about $1o0 million in
excess of the other estimates. This substantial differ-
ence is to be accounted for mainly by the following con-
siderations:
(1) Differences

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
20
(1) Differences in basis of computing Japan's gold
reserves under devaluation.
(2) Exports of gold subsequent to date of Jones'
estimate.
(3) Jones' incorrect procedure in including annual
gold production among resources at a given date.
(4) Jones' acceptance of official Japanese figures
of holdings of foreign securities on questionable basis
of yen computation, without due allowance for their
effective realisable value.
On the other hand, Japanese resources in the form
of foreign bank balances, which, at the date of Jones!
report, were found to be "negligible", appear to have
sinoe been built up to a level of from 55 to 65 million
as shown in the later estimates.
Checks made by the writer indicate that Japan's
total gold holdings are nearer the $300 million level
than the figure of $275 given in the Treasury Depart-
ment's memorandum. On the other hand, the amount which
may be regarded as "free" gold, in exoess of present
legal reserve requirements of the Bank of Japan, would
appear to be only about one half of the Treasury Depart-
ment's estimate of $75 million. Apart from these diminishing
stocks, future Japanese gold exports can be met from new

tember 6 in whioh you stated that among the principai
measures introduced by the Government at the session of
the Diet beginning September 3 was a bill revising the
Foreign Exchange Control Law and investing the Minister
of Finance with authority to require any Japanese sub-
jeot to liquidate all his assets in foreign countries
and transfer the proceeds so realized to the Bank of
Japan. According to your cable of September 15, this
measure was approved by the Diet.
Several questions arise with regard to this measure.
In the first place, we are wondering why it was considered
necessary to obtain special authorization from the Diet
for this step inasmuch as the necessary powers would
appear to have been already delegated to the Government
in the Foreign Exohange Control Law of 1933. You are
referred in this conneotion to article Iv of this law as
given 1n your Economio and Trade Note No. 146, September
6, 1937.
We are chiefly interested, however, in learning how
much foreign exchange Japan may expect to realize in this
wmy. In this conneotion the figures on Japanese owned
foreign currenoy seourities and balances abroad, col-
leoted and published at regular intervals by the Depart-
ment of Finanoe, are of interest. The latest and most
detailed statement of this sort in our files is that
given on page 217 of the Oriental Eoonomist for April
1937, whioh gives the figures as of September 30, 1936.
The Áugust lssue of the same publication makes a brier
reference to the figures as of April 30, 1937, but un-
fortunately only the gross total 1s given (see pp. 445-6).
You are requested to forward the figures for April 30 and
for all later dates as they become available in as much
detail as possible.
We should like to know if possible what rates of con-
version have been used in expressing Japanese owned foreign
assets

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
assets in terms of yen. The footnote to the table in
the April issue of the Oriental Economist 1s rather
imprecisely stated. It appears that the first two groups
shown 1n the table ("external loans" and "foreign securi-
ties") have been converted at the old mint parities,
namely 9.763 yen to the pound, 2.006 yen to the dollar,
and 6.387 yen to the French franc, etc. One might inter-
pret the note to mean, however, that "foreign securities"
expressed in dollars have been converted at 1.185 yen to
the dollar (or 84.4 cents to the yen). Furthermore, no
statement is made as to the rates used for converting the
third group -- "balances in foreign currencies and ad-
vances". We are aware that in your Special Weekly Finan-
cial Report of May 18, 1936, you state with regard to
these figures that they have all been converted at the
old mint par of exohange. It might be well, however, to
check again on this statement and make sure that it applies
especially to all three groups.
We also desire to know as much as possible as to the
composition of the figures in question, particularly with
reference to the liquidity of the assets. Thus 1t would
be helpful to have some idea as to the amount of American,
British, French and other readily marketable securities
and balances represented in these holdings. Burton Crane,
writing 1n the New York Times of September 25, states that
approximately $200,000,000 1s held 1n Government bonds of
the United States, Great Britain, and France". We do not
know the basis for this statement, but have our doubts as
to its accuracy. A detailed study of foreign investments
in the United States made by this Division indicates
strongly that Japanese holdings of American securities
are negligible. It is very 1mprobable, of course, that
you will be able to learn anything very definite on this
subject, but it might be possible to find out whether or
not the figures in question include any Chinese securities.
The Oriental Economist of April 1937 states that no Man-
churian obligations are included, but makes no reference
to China. The significance of Japan's foreign assets as
shown by the figures in question obviously depends to a
considerable degree on this factor.
It would also be interesting to know whether or not
the figures on "balances in foreign currencies and ad-
vances" include foreign assets of the Yokohama Specie
Bank and the Bank of Japan. In connection with these
balances and advanoes, you may be interested in the fig-
ures compiled and published by the United States Treasury
on

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
on capital movements between the United States and for-
eign countries. These figures are broken down geographi-
cally to some extent, but unfortunately the Far East is
given as one group and separate figures are not available
for Japan. According to this source, the Far Eastb short-
term assets in this country as reported by banks and
bankers have ranged as follows since the beginning of

rr

Cc
dividends due the Ford Motor Company in the United States,
2
I have the honor to report that Mr. Benjamin Kopf called
at the Embassy on December 23, 1937, to discuss the
assistance to be rendered in aocordance with the Department's
F/FG
instruotion
141

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
instruction under acknowledgment.
Mr. Kopf stated that when he made his approach to
the Exchange Control Commission with a view to expediting
permission to remit the total of approximately three million
yen due the parent company in the United States as dividends
to June 30, 1937, he was told that if he would purchase
national bonds to the value of one million yen he would
be granted permission to remit one million yen immediately
and permission to remit an additional million about
January 1. Mr. Kopf stated that after due consideration he
decided to purchase one million yen worth of bonds. He
added thet he anticipated no difriculty in obtaining the
further million promised for January end that he had the
promise of the Exchange Control Commission that in addition
they would give consideration to the transfer of approximately
three million yen representing dividends due the parent
company for the six months ending December 31, 1937. Mr.
Kopf then requested the Embassy to do nothing for the
present saying he did not anticipate any difficulty in the
immediate future, but that he might call upon us later on if
the matter did not work out as well as expected.
Mr. Kopf, when questioned whether the Government had
specified the type of bonds he was to purchase, replied
that no particular type of national bonds was mentioned.
However, the bonds purchased were those issued to finance
the China incident.
The only other case that has come to the attention
of

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
of the Embassy where the Exchange Control Conmission
proposed the purchase of national bonds is that of the
Singer Sewing Machine Company. The representative of
the Singer Sewing Machine Company states that some
time ago he was informed that he would be granted
permission to remit funds if national bonds were
purchased. The Singer Sewing Machine Company, however,
has not purchased any bonds to date, and while the
company received in October last a permit to remit thirty-
seven thousand pounds sterling, they have on hand
approximately three and one half million yen for which
permission to remit has not been granted to date by
the Exchange Control Commission.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

9

6

In March, April and May of 1937 the Association
sold to Japanese merchants and mills for shipment in
MAR 2 8 1938
September, October and November, 1937, 13,450 bales of
FILED
American cotton, the sales value being $1,072,093.
For several months the Japanese Government held up re-
imbursements for the cotton in question, promising ex-
F/A
change permits at a later date in order to liquidate
the old contracts as well as the exchange permits for

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.98)

new business at the current market price. The Japanese
Government now expresses its willingness to permit the
granting of exchenge permits for previous shipments of
cotton at the present market price but refuses permits
for the margin payments owed to American shippers, that
is, the difference between the sales price and the pres-
ent price of cotton. The Government is willing to grant
exchange permits to the Association for the present
price of $746,867 but not for the difference between
this sum and the sales velue of $1,072,093.
Mr. Saer suggests that the Department might take
steps to assist the Association in obtaining exchange
permits for the full amount due the Association.
Mr. Saer was told that there was little this Gov-
ernment was in position to do in regard to foreign ex-
change control except to be on the alert for discrimina-
tion in its administration but thet the Department would
consider the question of what could be done in this
particular case.
Incidental to the main question Mr. Jones asked
1f there was any probability that American cotton shippers

The anewer was emphatically in the negative. Japanese
purchasers are now attempting to persuade American
shippers

Last fall Mr. Orvis asked the Department's assistance in
obtaining the release in Japan of foreign exchange for the
transfer into dollars of yen funds owing to himself. The
Department instructed the Embassy in Tokyo to point out to
the Foreign Office the delay in granting exchange permits
to Mr. Orvis' correspondent in Japan. The Embassy replied,
and Mr. Orvis was so informed, that exchange had been granted
to the extent of Yen 281,000.
Mr. Orvis now discloses that his total funds blocked in
Japan amount to a much larger figure and wishes the Depart-
F
ment so to inform the Japanese authorities.
A
Mr. Jones suggested that Mr. Orvis put his facts and
figures in writing in the form of a letter to the Department,
whereupon the Department would give due consideration to his
request.

=== Page 161 === (confidence 1.00)
COPY
MARUZEN COMPANY, LTD.
P.0.Box 605 Tokyo Central
Tokyo, Japan
March 17, 1938
The American Institute of Physics
New York
Gentlemen:
According to our existing arrangements, it is
now over the time to settle your statements for monthly
accounts.
Since the beginning of this year, we have applied
to the fiscal authorities for the settlement, but to our
great regret, the remittance permit has not yet been given.
The cause of the delay is that it was just the budget-making
period and the Diet is still in session and further the
authorities dealt with our application in parallel with
other numerous applications on general imports.
Importation and payment for foreign books and
periodicals, as well as other articles, are made under
Foreign xchange Control Law and it is inevitably apt to
cause some delay in our payment owing to necessary pro-
cedures on the part of the authorities in giving permission.
However, the authorities recognize foreign books
and periodicals as essential and important articles and are
strictly following the policy not to prohibit or restrict
their importation except only a small portion which is quite
unurgent. As to payment for those already imported, the
authorities affirm that they will endeavor to give permission
for them as soon as possible.
We are also doing everything in our power to get the
permission, and upon getting it we will promptly remit to you.
Under the circumstances, you will please appreciate
the above, and allow us some extension of time in payment.
Assuring you of our best attention to your accounts,
Yours very truly
(Signed) MARUZEN COMPANY Ltd.

My dear Mr. Barton:
The receipt is ackmowledged of your letter of
April 12, 1938, describing the difficulties you are
89
having in receiving paymont of an account from the
4.5
Maruzen Company, Limited, in Japan, and asking for
5151/77
certain information in regard to the operation of the
Japanese Foreign Exchange Control Law.
According to the Foreign Exchange Control Law of
Japan, as amended, the purchase of foreign exchange for
offecting the financial settlement necessary to the im-
portation of foreign goods into Japan may not take
place except with the permission of the Minister of
Finance. In fact, applications must be made to and are
considered by the Exchange Control Board within the
F/A
Ministry of Pinance.
Various
Mr. Henry A. Barton,
Director,
Ameriean Institute of Physios, Incorporated,
175 Fifth Avenue,
Now York, New York.

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
Various instances of delay in the granting of permits
by the Exchange Control Board have come to the attention of
the Department. These delays appear to arise from a variety
of causes, inoluding the desire of the Japanese Government
to discourage "unnecessary" imports, the desire to balance
as far as possible at any given time outgo and income in
terms of exchange, and inevitable crowding of the adminis-
trative machinery. The Department is not aware of any
policy which the Japanese Government may have in regard to
the granting of exchange permits for imports of books; but
in view of the fact that the books have already entered
Japan and in view of the smallness of the account, it feels
that the delay may be of an administrative character and
sees no reason for assuming that there is no possibility
that an exchange permit will be granted.
The Department appreciates the significance of the sum
in question to your organization. Should there occur undue
further delay in this matter the Department upon request
will ask 1ts representatives in Japan to point out the delay
to the appropriate Japanese authorities and to inquire as to
when the granting of the permit in question may be expected.
Sincerely yours,
OR
For the Seoretary of State:
