JAPAN'S FOREIGN EXCHANGE RESOURCES
AT THE END OF 1937
Japan's economic capacity to continue military
894.5151
operations in China until she can attain the terms of
settlement or the controls she desires 1s less a ques-
tion of internal than of external financing. Although
Japan 1s not dependent today, as in the war of 1904-5
894.5151/
with Russia, on the importation of munitions and war
equipment, she is more vitally dependent than then on
the importation of raw materials, both for war purposes
directly and for her export manufacturing industries
8
(other than silk). In the absence of foreign loans
4
(by whioh Japan was able to finance nearly two thirds
of the total cost of her war with Russia) Japan's
capacity to procure essential imports in excess of the
proceeds of ourrent exports depends on her actual and
potential resources available as foreign exchange.
For the first eleven months of 1937 Japan's excess
of imports over exports amounted to 647 million yen,
compared to 145 mill1on yen in the same period of 1936.
This external trade deficit has been more than covered
by
F/FG

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.97)
-2
by exports of gold and silver. Continuance of hostili-
ties through 1938 (even in the absence of foreign inter
vention) will almost certainly involve a larger monthly
trade defioit. This paper examines the extent and form
of exohange resources still available to Japan for bal-
ancing her external accounts, and, by implication, her
capacity to continue the struggle until her objectives
are achieved. The estimates presented here are based
on a ohecking and comparison of figures contained in a
y2
report prepared in the State Department in mid-Septem-
ber, and in a confidential memorandum of the Treasury
Department under date of December 13, supplemented by
later information derived from various official and
semi-cfficial sources.
Gold Resources
At the end of February 1937 the gold reserves of
the Bank of Japan had reached a peak level of $469 mil-
lion. Shipments of gold abroad to support the yen which
commenced in March were sufficiently in excess of new
gold purohases to bring the Bank's holdings down to
$386 million by August 25, when devaluation of the yen
(to 38.66 peroent of its former gold content) became
effeotive. In conneotion therewith gold to the amount
of

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
3
of 80l million revalued yen, or $26l million, was left
in the Bank of Japan as a fixed currency reserve, both
against its own note circulation and that of the Banks
of Chosen and Taiwan, whose gold holdings were turned
over at that time to the Bank of Japan. The remaining
gold, equivalent to $134 million, was allocated to the
Government's "Gold Fund Special Account", which was
designed to be employed for maintaining the exchange
value of the yen at 14 pence, or between 28 and 29
cents. It is from this special Government fund that
all gold shipments have been made since devaluation
became effeotive, and it is this fund which has since
absorbed all new Government purchases of gold. Since
reported shipments of gold from Japan between August 25
and the end of the year have amounted to $1lo million,
the indioated year-end gold holdings in the Government's
special fund is $24 million, plus whatever new purchases
of gold may have been added. As purchases through the
Bank of Japan of newly mined gold averaged about 120,000
ounces, or $4.2 million a month during the first half of
1937, it is possible that the fund may have acquired
somewhere between 15 and 20 million dollars in new gold
since August. Thus, with indicated holdings of between
40 and 45 million dollars in the Government's secret
fund

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
4
fund and with the Bank of Japan's gold reserves of $261
million still intact, Japan woula appear to have command
of not less than $300 million in gold at the end of 1937.
Future gold shipments for supporting the yen or
building up Japanese balances abroad may be met to a
limited extent from the small residual holdings in the
Government's gold fund and from new production. Gold
produotion in the Japanese Empire has approximately
doubled since 1931, being estimated at about $50 mil-
l1on for l937, with Chosen and Taiwan supplying slightly
more than Japan proper. Despite Government efforts to
expand gold output through higher yen buying prices and
subsidies to approved prospecting, drilling and milling
enterprises, as provided under the Gold Production Law
of last August, it 1s doubtful if any further appreciable
increase can be realised. Nevertheless, Japan can prob-
ably count on at least $4.5 million of new gold a month
being available during 1938.
Gold exports in excess of current production--and
the former were nearly five times as large as the latter
during 1937--would, with the exhaustion of the remaining
holdings in the Government's fund, involve drawing upon
the gold reserves of the Bank of Japan, which, sinoe the
devaluation date, have been maintained intact at 801
m1ll1on

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.98)
-5
million yen ($26l million). Under existing law the
Bank's note circulation, in excess of the fiduciary lim-
it of 1 billion yen, 1s required to be covered 100 per-
cent by specie (in effect, by gold). Since the Bank's
note circulation has risen from 1,580 million yen at
the end of August to 1,87? million at the end of Novem-
does not possess any "free" gold. The necessity of
changing the present currency law would not of course
be an effeotive impediment to drawing upon the Bank's
gold reserves for export purposes should the situation
demand it. The fact is that Japan possesses an ultimate
gold reserve in excess of the total amount exported dur-
ing 1937. Under emergency a few additional million in
gold might be reoovered through the mobilisation and
melting down of gold articles, as in Italy during the
Ethiopian campaign.
Although the gold output of Manchukuo--where the
Manchurian Gold Mining monopoly is pursuing a program
of aggressive exploitation--1s acquired by the Bank of
Manchou
The gold reserve of the Bank of Japan also indireotly
supports the note ciroulation of the Banks of Chosen
and Taiwan, whose own gold holdings of some $16 mil-
lion were turned over to the Bank of Japan in exohange
for notes of the latter at the time of yen devalua-
tion.

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.97)
-6-
Manchou, such gold could be used to finance in part
imports for the use of the Kwantung and Manchukuo armies.
Gold production 1n Manchukuo in 1937 1s estimated at 15
million yen, or about 4 1/2 million dollars, and can
doubtless bo 1ncreased.
Foreign Exchange Balances Held Abroad
The Yokohama Specie Bank, which dominates Japanese
foreign exchange transactions, normally maintains sub-
stantial credit balances in London and New York for
financing Japanese imports. The heavy excess import
movement during the first eight months of 1937, and
the reluctance of British and American banks and ex-
porters to extend the credits desired by Japanese clients
after the development of hostilities, had caused Japanese
balanoes in London and New York to be sharply reduced,
and neoessitated extensive gold shipments to the United
States for the oreation of dollar and sterling exchange.
A cheok of Japanese bank balances in New York made by
the United States Treasury in mid-September indicated a
net overdraft of $1 million. Subsequent check-ups re-
vealed net balances of $45 million at the end of November
and $43 mill1on at the end of December. It is not defi-
nitely known what balances are held to Japanese account
in

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
-7
in London, but the Treasury estimates them to be between
2 and 4 million sterling. Thus at the beginning of 1938
Japan appears to have at her command between 50 and 60
million dollars in foreign exchange balances abroad.
The improvement in this position since early Sep-
tember has been made possible, partly by subsequent gold
shipments to the United States exceeding $1o0 million,
and partly by heavy curtailments in Japanese 1mports
under the emergency import and exchange restrictions
instituted in October, accompanied by a seasonal rise
in Japanese exports. For the three months September-
November, Japan's foreign trade showed an export surplus
of 128 million yen, or $35 million, while United States
imports from Japan came within $6.5 million of balancing
American exports to that country. Thus, although foreign
loans have not been available to her, Japan enters the
new year with a fairly substantial supply of ready for
eign exohange and with the yen quoted above 29 cents in
New York.
Foreign Seourity Holdings
All holdings of foreign securities at home or abroad
by Japanese nationals are required to be registered with
the Japanese Ministry of Finance, and under an ordinance
of

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
of September 9, 1937, the Government may order a person
to sell to the Bank of Japan or other designated agency
any seourities expressed in foreign currencies held in
the name of such national. The actual extent, character
and realisable value of such holdings by Japanese insti-
tutions and individuals are difficult to determine. The
Ministry of Finance reported that holdings at home and
abroad of foreign government, municipal and corporate
securities by Japanese nationals, as of April 1937,
totaled 389 million yen (at face value of bonds converted
at mint par of exchange). If the latter is taken as the
old rate of 1 yen = 49.86 cents, prior to both dollar
and yen devaluation, these holdings would be equivalent
to nearly $200 million. Although the Ministry states
that its figures do not include Manchukuo Government
bonds, it is believed that a large proportion of the
total consists of Chinese obligations, which may be
regarded as having a negligible liquidating value. In-
vestigations made by the United States Treasury Depart-
ment as to the extent of Japanese holdings of dollar
securities are inconolusive but indicate a possible
range of from 25 to 75 million dollars. It 1s to be
noted that the last detailed report 1ssued by the Japan-
ese Ministry of Finance (as of September 30, 1936) showed
that

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.98)
一9
that 87 peroent of all Japanese-owned foreign securi-
ties were held abroad, the proportion being as high as
95 percent in the case of national government bonds.
To the extent that these seourities are held by Japanese
banks or insurance institutions in the United States,
their liquidation in exchange for yen securities or
oredits is obviously limited by the necessity of main-
taining dollar assets against the liabilities of such
institutions to their depositors and policy holders.
It would appear, therefore, that the amount of
foreign exohange whioh might be realised through the
liquidation of Japanese-owned foreign securities is very
muoh less than that indicated by the inclusive, nominal
holdings as reported in Japan. It is doubtful if more
than from 75 to 80 million dollars could be effectively
obtained through the liquidation of such assets.
Japanese External Bonds
In recent years there has been considerable repatria-
tion of Japanese publio and corporate bonds issued in
foreign currencies prior to 1930. According to the
Ministry of Finance, the nominal value of such holdings
by Japanese nationals,as of September 30, 1936, amounted
to  724 million yen (at old par of exchange), or about
$360 million. Any extended selling of these obligations
1n

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.98)
101
in the foreign markets in which they are listed or traded,
as a means of realising foreign exchange, would of course
seriously depress their market value in view of the re-
stricted acoeptability of suoh securities under existing
conditions. As it 1s, even such a gilt-edge foreign
bond as Japanese Government 6 1/2 external loan, upon
which debt service has been fully maintained and which
was eelling at or above par in New York in the early
part of 1937, has been quoted during the year as low as
67 1/8, although year-end quotations have ranged between
75 and 80. It 1s possible that through cautious or
secret selling of such securities, Japan might be able
to realise something like 20 to 25 million dollars from
this source.

Japan's Foreign Exchange Requirements
The extent to which Japan may have to draw upon
her estimated actual and potential foreign exchange
resources of from 475 to 500 million dollars during
1938 depends upon the oourse of her balance of trade
and other invisible international transactions.
As previously noted, Japan's adverse merchandise
trade balance for the first eleven months of 1937
amounted to 647 mill1on yen, and may be slightly less
for the year as a whole. Excluding trade with Manchukuo,
Kwantung Territory and China, which, being conducted
more or less on a yen or silver basis, does not yield
free foreign exchange commensurate with Japan's reported
favorable trade balance with these countries (amounting
to 230 million yen for the first nine months of 1937),
Japan's effective external trade deficit for the year
may be estimated in the neighborhood of 900 million
yen, or about $260 million.
Ordinarily Japan's invisible trade (exclusive of
capital movements) shows a net credit balance as an
offset to her excess of merchandise imports. Detailed
figures of Japan's invisible trade in 1936, recently
issued by the Ministry of Finance, showed a net excess
of "ordinary" receipts over payments amounting to 233
mi1l1on

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
2
million yen.
In view of the disappearance of returns
from Japanese direct investments and enterprises in
China since the outbreak of hostilities, of the shrink-
age in foreign exchange earnings of Japanese shipping
through diversion to war transport purposes, of the
falling-off in foreign tourist expenditures, and of the
presumable inorease in undisclosed Government expendi-
tures abroad, it is doubtful if Japan's ordinary invisible
trade in 1937 will provide any appreciable offset to her
external trade deficit. If hostilities are continued
through 1938, a negative balance may be expected on
invisible account.
During 1937 Japan shipped gold abroad to the extent
of $245 million, with silver exports estimated at $20
million, while there wes doubtless some liquidation of
Japanese holdings of foreign securities. In view of the
bank belances built up in New York, amounting as noted
to $43 million at the end of the year, these exports of
speoie and securities were apparently not fully required
to meet Japan's adverse trade balance in 1937. For the
year as a whole it appears that her foreign exchange
reserves
1
"Ordinary" receipts on invisible account inolude
interest and dividends on foreign securities, income
from undertakings abroad, shipping and insurance
receipts, expenditures of foreign tourists, resi-
dents, governments, eto.

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
13
reserves were drawn upon at an average monthly rate of
from $20 to $25 million.
Japan's heaviest purchases abroad were made before
the development of hostilities on a major scale. The
building up of large reserve stocks of war and industrial
raw materials purchased at high prices was reflected in
a cumulative trade deficit of 775 million yen by the end
of August. Since then, by drawing on these reserve
stocks, by drastic controls of imports and exchange, by
curtailing domestic consumption, and by vigorously
pushing export sales, Japan has been able to show a net
export surplus of 128 million yen for the three months
September through November--e result to which lower
prices of imported raw materials have also been a con-
tributing factor.
The trade outlook for Japan during the early part
of 1938 1s less favorable. The necessity of replenish-
ing depleted stocks of raw materials (especially of
cotton, wool, pulp and sugar), of continued heavy
imports of petroleum, metals, machine tools and motor
truoks, if military operations are to be maintained on
the present scale, and the difficulties of sustaining
exports in the face of restrictions on imports of
materials entering into manufaotures for export, of
rising

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
rising domestic costs, of the diversion of labor and
capital into war purposes and of developing anti-Japanese
boycott movements abroad; all these factors suggest that
Japan may have to draw rather heavily upon her exohange
reserves in the coming months if the yen is to be main-
tained at its present level.
Barring foreign economic or military intervention,
the situation cannot, however, be regarded as an
imminently critical one for Japan. With nearly $1o0
million immediately available in the form of foreign
bank balances and Government-held gold, with another
$260 mill1on in gold in the Bank of Japan, and with a
possible $100 million realisable through the liquida-
tion of Japanese-held foreign currency securities,
Japan could potentially carry on through the greater
part of 1938, even if losing gold or foreign exchange
at double the apparent monthly rate throughout 1937.
While it is not likely that Japan would pursue a war
of aggression to the point of completely exhausting
her ourrenoy reserves and liquid foreign assets, the
strain upon her financial resources does not yet appear
to be sufficiently serious to compel any imminent de-
fleotion of her objeotives or efforts in China--at least
so long as no foreign intervention develops.

Sir:
Enclosed is a newspaper account of a proposed arrange-
FILED
1/
ment between the American moving picture industry and the
Japanese Government to permit the importation of American
moving picture films into Japan in spite of present re-
strictions growing out of foreign exchange difficulties.
The Embassy has been informed by a representative of one of
the American companies that the account, although premature,
is approximately correct. He states that the plan was
proposed by the American companies, that he believes that
the Japanese Government has decided to accept it, and that
formal

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
formel approval is simply a matter of time and certain
formalities.
According to the plan the American companies would be
permitted to import into Japan a fixed quantity of films,
and as soon as payment is made in yen in Japan in the ordinary
commercial procedure the full amounts of such payments would
be converted into American dollars at the prevailing rate(29
American cents to a yen) and transferred to the Yokohama
Specie Bank in the United States, there to be held in dollar
balances without interest for three years, at the conclusion
of which to be payable to the respective American companies.
The American companies a parently believe that the American
control of foreign bank branches located within the United
States is adequate safeguard for this credit transaction.
The Embassy is informed that the quantity of American
films which could be imported if this arrangement goes into
effect is some 60% of the quantity which is imported from
the United States in normal times. The arrangement should
therefore not be interpreted as opening the country to the
usual film importations.
Although the arrangement so far discussed has had to do
only with purchases from the United States, it is presumed
that a similar arrangement would be arrived at for other
countries.
The proposal is of particular interest as showing the
readiness of American business to arrive at credit arrange-
ments, in return for business. In the present instance the
arrangement makes way for certain luxury imports into Japan
whioh would otherwise not be made. It is aside from, and
doos

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
does not relieve, the necessity which Japan faces of finding
foreign exchange to pay for imports of essentials.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

E F THE ADMISER ON
ULPARIMENI OF SIAIL
CONUMIC AFFARS
Japan's Foreign Exchange Position
IMENT OF STATE
The pressure on Japan's foreign exchange resources
arising from its war nvolvements is sinificantly reflected
in the announced decision of tne Japanese Cabinet on July 19
to authorize the export of 300 million yen in gold from the
Bank of Japan's present gold reserves of 8ol million yen for
894.5151
the creation abroad of a "foreign exchange revolving fund",
to be used for the purchase of raw materials essential to
Japan's export industries.
This action implies: (I)
Exhaustion of the Government's
8
own gold holdings, (II) determination to maintain the exist-
6
ing gold exchange rate of the yen at expense of currency
reserves; (IIl) nece:sity of paying for essential imports on
cash basis; (Iv) renewed effort to restore Japan's falling
export trade as means of currently financing indispensable
imports.
I
Since the formal devaluation of the yen on August 25,
F/FG
1937, all gold exports from Japan have come from the Gov-
ernment's

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.97)
-2 -
ernment's secret gold fund, to which approximately 400
million yen ($130 million) in gold was transferred at that
time (representing the adjusted increment arising from
revaluation of the gold stocks of the Banks of Japan,
Chosen and Taiwan) and into which have passed all subse-
quent Government purchases of domestically mined and old
go1d.
Practically all gold shipments from Japan since the
movement commenced in March 1937, have been consigned to
the San Franciso branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank. Dur-
ing 1937 exports of gola from Japan to the United States
totaled $245 million, of which $1l6 million was received
subsequent to August when the yen was devalued, and the
Government's Golc Fund Special Account was established.
During 1938 the amount of gold shipped to date to this
country has amounted to $84.3 million. Thus total gold
shipments to the United States since last August have
aggregated $200 million. These have bee.: met presumably
by using up the Treasury's yen devaluation gold profits
($130 million) and by the export of Government purchases
of domestically mined and old gold. According to the Vice-
Governor of the Bank of Japan, Government purchases in 1937
of

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.98)
一3-
of newly mined gold amounted to l8l million yen, equivalent
to $52 million or a monthly average of $4.5 million. A1-
though strenuous efforts have been made to expand gold pro-
duction in the Empire and Manchukuo through subsidizing
prospecting and drilling operations and by successive ad-
vances in the Goveriment's monopoly ourchase orice, it is
doubtful if the output in l938 will be much in excess of
$60 million. Assuming Government purchases of newly mined
gold at an average monthly rate of $5 million since last
August, the remainder of the gold exoorted to the United
States (approximately s20 million) must have come from our-
chases or donations of gold in the form of privately neld
gold coins, jewelry, ornaments, etc. An active campaign
is peing waged to increase acquisitions fron such sources,
which might conceivably be carried to the extent of stripping
gold decorations frcm tenples and shrines.
Although the amount of old gold that might be thus
mobilized is uncertain (Japanese potential estimates range
from 273 million to 1 billion yen), it appears clear that
the Government's gold fund derived from devaluation profits
has been completely exhausted, and tnat gold shipments to
the United States during the current year, averaging $14
million monthly, have been equivalent to nearly three times
the

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
the average monthly rate of old production in the Empire.
At any rate the Government has now found it necessary to
draw upon the gold currency reserves of the Bank of Japan
which have hitherto remained intact since devaluation of
the yen.
II
The action taken by the Cabinet tris week reflects the
determination to maintain the pegged exchange rate of the
yen at 14 pence, or 28 to 29 cents (depending on the ster-
ling-dollar cross rate), at the expense of the Bank of
Japan's specie reserves. A fall in the excnange value of
the yen at this time would automatically increase the yen
cost of Japan's essential imports, with doubtful effect
in stimulating its exports. However, under existing con-
trols, the retention of a large dormant gold currency re-
serve is unnecessary to maintain the internal value of the
yen. Utilization of the Bank's reserves for supporting
the external value of the yen was anticipated in the
action taken last February in raising the fiduciary
limit of the Bank's note circulation from l000 million
to 1,700 million yen. The transfer of 300 million yen
of the Bank's total gold reserves of 80l million yen will
still leave in its possession 500 million in gold, which
with

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
with note circulation ranging currently in the neighborhood
of 2000 million yen, would represent a 25 percent gold
coverage. (The Reichsbank has barely more than 1 percent
gold coverage against its circulation). Inasmuch, however,
as the Bank of Japan's note circulation is bound to expand
under continued Government deficit financing, further deple-
tion of its currency reserves foreshadows, after the termina-
tion of the present war crisis, either a new depreciation of
the yen, or continuance of rigid exchange controls as in
Germany and Italy.
III
The decision to use 300 million yen ($98 million)* of
the Bank of Japan's gold for establishing balances abroad
for the purchase of raw materials essential to Japan's
export industries, is indicative of the difficulties that
Japanese importers are experiencing in obtaining even or-
dinary commercial credits. Recent failure of the Finance
Ministry in many cases to furnish exchange permits even
where corresponding import permits had been duly issued,
have caused most exporters in the United States and else-
where to insist either on cash payment or on irrevocable
letters of credit. This situation has been creating serious
The figure of $84 million appears in press reports based on
current yen exchange rate of 28 cents. The Bank of Japan's
gold holdings were revalued, however, at the rate of 290 milli-
grams to the yen, equivalent to 32.63 cents.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
difficulties for Japanese exporters de endent on imported
raw materials, and threatens to interfere with the prompt
uelivery of essential military imports. In the absence
of foreign loans or credits, therefore, Japan has no al-
ternative but to convert a substantial part of its currency
reserves into cash balances abroad.
IV
The Cabinet's decision implies an aggressive effort
to restore Japan's falling export trace, especially in
cotton textiles which during the first half of tnis year
have been 18 percent below that of the corresponding period
of l937, while total exports have been 20 vercent less in
value. Under recent regulat ions imports of raw co ton can
be used only in manufacture for export or military con-
sumption. Although the new foreign exchange fund may re-
lieve existing difficulties of Japanese exporters in filling
foreign orders arising from scarcity of essential imported
raw materials,such financial facilities will not serve
in themselves to regain trade lost due to anti-Japanese
sentiment or recession conditions abroad. It is not
unlikely that new and enlarged export subsidies may be
resorted to as a means of offsetting rising operating costs
in Japan's export industries.
As

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
As announced by the Japanese Finance Minister it is
intended that drafis drawn against tne revolving foreign
exchange fund for purchases of raw materials abroad shall
be replaced within a fixed period by the proceeds of bills
arising from exports of Japanese goods manufactured frcm
such materials. If this import-export link plan is ad-
hered to, it is difficult to see how the necessary foreign
exchange can be obtained for imports of essential war
materials (which by neir nature cannot be re-exported)
without depleting the fund.
Although Japanese inports during the first nalf of
this year have been reduced to a reater extent than
Japanese exports (31 percent as against 20 percent in
relation to the corresponding period of l937), the re-
corded excess of imports in the first six months of this
year has nevertheless amounted to 191 million yen. The
true trade deficit is undoubtedly considerably larger
than the indicated figure, since direct Government pur-
chases of oil for the military forces (and probably for
other war equipment as well) are not included in the
official import returns. Moreover, the foreign excnange
earnings of Japan's merchant marine - an important item
in its invisible export account - have been heavily re-
duced

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.95)
-8-
duced through the extensive diversion of its snipping to
military transport uses. Unless therefore a greater ex-
pansion can be achieved tnan appears likely in Japan's
exports - especially of such items as silk, marine products,
pottery, et cetera, wnich are not dependent on imported
raw materials - it would seem that continued imports of
military materials on the existing scale can only be
maintained - in the absence of foreign credits - by fur-
ther depletion of Japan's reduced gold resources.
1

No. 23/C1
Monsieur l'Ambassadeur,
Me rérérant à ma lettre No. 17/Cg, en date du 16
juin dernier, par laquelle j'ai eu l'honneur d'accuser
réoeption à Votre Excellence de sa lettre No. 40 et
datée du 31 mai dernier, proposant une simplification
des formalités requises pour le payement effectué à
l'étranger par les Missions diplomatiques étrangères
au Japon, je m'empresse de porter à sa connaissance
oe qui suit :
De l'enquête faite à la réception de le lettre de
Votre Excellence, il résulte qu'aucun retard n'a été
apporté à dessein dans l'octroi des autorisetions
demandées, comme il était signalé dans sa lettre.
Toutefois, à la suite de délibérations aved le  inis-
tère des Finances en vue d'établir, du point de vue de
la courtoisie internationale, un régime exceptionnel,
il a été décidé que l'envoi de fonds de caractère public
par les Ambassades, Légations et Consulats étrangers
au Japon à leurs Pays respectifs ne serait pas désormais
subordonné à 1'autorisation exigée par l'Arrêté No. 7
du Ministère des Finances, demandant toutefois seulement
la notification par trois mois, au Ministère des
Finances par 1'intermédiaire du Ministère des Affaires
étrangères, du montant total des fonds envoyés pour
cette période, cette notification est regardée comme
tenant lieu de celle prévue à l'article 23 dudit Arrêté
ministériel No. 7.
En ce qui concerne l'envoi à l'étranger de fonds
personnels des Chefs de Mission et des membres des
Ambassades, Légations et Consulats étrangers au Japon
(excepté les Consulats Honoraires), il était autorisé
jusqu'ici, toutes les fois qu'il a été justifié, sans
aucune difficulté. Cependant, on a l'intention de leur
accorder désormais plus de facilités pour l'envoi de
fonds de ce genre, en en donnant l'autorisation
globalement a l'envoi éventuel de fonds pour une
période relativement longue, à la seule condition que
la demande d'autorisation soit accompagnée d'une men-
tion détaillée des fonds envoyés dans le passé par le
requérant.
Le Ministère des Finances, sentant la nécessité
de se bien renseigner sur les circonstances réelles
pour accorder le nouveau traitement simplirié, vient de
m'exprimer le désir de connaitre, même d'une manière
approrimative, le montant total des droits perqus et
onvoyés en Belgique pendant l937 (année du calendrier)
par l'Ambassade et les Consulats de Belgique au Japon
(Ce renseignement pour 193s a été déjà fourni).
Je

-2-

de vouloir bien faire parvenir, en tenant compte de
oe qui a motivé ledit traitement bienveillant, ce
renseignement le plus rapidement possible au Ministère
des Affaires étrangères.
Veuillez agréer, Monsieur l'Ambassadeur, les
assurances renouvelées de ma très haute considération.
(L.S.) Ministre des Affaires

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
Annex to Circular Note No. 9.
THE GADMUSHO
TOKYO
Translation.
July 16, 1938
No. 23 C1.
Mr. Ambassador:
Referring to my letter no. 17 Cg. dated June 16
last, in which I had the honor to acknowledge the re-
ceipt of Your Excellency's note no. 40, dated May 31
last, proposing a simplification of the formalities
required for payments made abroad by diplomatic missions
in. Japan, I hasten to inform Your Excellency as follows:
According to inquiries made following the receipt
of Your Excellency's letter, it appears that no hin-
drance has knowingly been put forward in the matter of
the authorizations requested, as was indicated in Your
Excellency's note. However, following conversations
with the Ministry of Finance with a view to establishing,
from a point of view of international courtesy, an
exceptional régime, it has been decided that the remit-
tance of funds of a public character by the foreign
Embassies, Legations, and Consulates in Japan to their
respective countries henceforth will not be subject to
the authorization required by Order No. 7 of the Ministry
of Finance, requiring, however, only the notification
overy three months to the Ministry of Finance through
the intermediary of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of
the total sum of the funds remitted during this period;
this notifioation is regarded as replacing that en-
visaged in Article 23 of the aforesaid ministerial
Order No. 7.
Concerning

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Concerning the remittance abroad of the personal
funds of Chiefs of Mission and members of the Embassies,
Legations, and foreign Consulates in Japan (excepting
honorary Consulates), this has been authorized up to
the present, in every case where the action has been
found to be justified, ith no difficulty whatever.
However, it is intended in the future to grant them
greater facilities for the remittance abroad of funds
of this nature, by giving inclusive authorization for
the future remittance of funds for a relatively long
period on the sole condition that the request for au-
thorization be accompanied by a detailed account of the
funds sent in the past by the applicant.
The Ministry of Finance feeline it necessary to
keep itself well informed of the actual circumstances
in order to grant the new simplified treatment, has
expressed to me the desire to be informed, even approxi-
mately, of the total sums collected and sent to Belgium
during the calendar year 1937 by the Belgian Embassy and
Consulates in Japan. (This information for 1936 has
already been furnished.)
I will aocording y be very grateful to Your Ex-
cellenoy if you will be so kind as to furnish in con-
sideration of the notification of the aforementioned
friendly treatment, this information at the earliest
possible moment to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs.
Please accept, etc...
(soal)
Minister for Foreign Affairs

was acquired  the open mrket subsequent
to that time after the rates declined. The
Borden Company apparently wishes to put on
record the fact that if exchange control is
established at Shanghai by either the Chin-
ese National Government or by Japanese
authorities and if it becomes obligatory to
resell all exchange to a central bank at
the official rate of exchange, the company
would suffer a very substential loss.

TE BORDE COI PANY

Shanchni, China, July 21, 1938.
American Consulote-General.
Shanghai.
Attention: Mr. R. P. Butrick
Sirs:-
In connection ith our conversation vith :r.
Butric: on the l9th, reletive to the possible danger
of Shenghai exports being subjected to controlled
exchange, we vich to point out the disastrous effects
such export regulaticns vould heve on our business.
We are packers and exporters of Frozen end Dried
Egg Products (largely Frozen Licuid Egg) end heve stocks
in our cold storage to t e vlue of over U"6c0,000.00.
These goods have been sold b. The Amos Bird
Division of Shenghei to the Borden Co pan of Yew York,
who have sold the esgs on contracts taken in March/April
to buyers abroad, with monthly deliveries during the year.
The exchange has been settled as it was not possible to
borrow local funds  ithout the settling of exchange. A
considerable portion of these oods were packed at ex-
change rate of 292 and then with the decline of the
Chinese Dollar the price of raw suplies increased.
If we should be required to resell to the Govern-
ment bank at controlled rate of exchange of 292 in order
to secure export permit, we would be forced to purchase
back U.S. Dollars at market rate of 184, which would
establish for us a loss of approximately 40%.
As the refrigerated steamer space for abroad is
limited, it would require approximately four months to
move our stocks, coupled with the fact that the high
storage expenses abroad would cause a fearful loss.
We know that the American Consul will do all in
their power to protect our interests, especially in pro-
testing the retroactive features of any regulations for
exchange control on exports, as these transactions have
been entirely completed except as to shipment. We will
appreciate any information and advice which you may have
from time to time so that we may advise our Principals
accordingly.
Respectfully yours,
THE BORDON COMPANY

My dear Sir:
Mr. Rothschild, our representative in Chefoo,
China reports to us that every day it is getting more
difficult to operate the business on account of the Japanese
restrictions on the money used in this territory.
894.5151/
I am enclosing copy of the last report from
393.
Mr. Rothschild explaining the way the Japanese are manipulating
the situation. They refuse to accept Shanghai exchange, which
is also Japanese territory, for customs duty and insist on
obtaining Northern Chinese currenoy - and as soon as they
secure same, they put it out of circulation.
In the meantime, they are asking the Chinese merchants
6
und workers in the country to accept Japanese control of exchange,
5
which on the other hand they are unable to do because this
territory is controlled by the Chinese guerillas who prevent
the dealing in such exchange.
The regular Northern Chinese xchange is quoted at
19 to the U.S. dollar against 29 for vhe new Japanese type
of Chinese exchange. This evidently is their plan in orde to
eventually make the yen the medium of exchange in this tertorys
and this is one of the reasons, if the Japanese control Chtna, 
why it would be most detrimental to all American firms opefating
there.
S
For example, when I was in China in 1930, it was
more difficult for a foreigner to enter and do business in
Manchukuo than it was in Tokyo or Yokohoma. If the Japanese
are permitted to continue this method of operation, it would be
equivalent to the American manufacturers as an actyal blockade
of imports. I therefore respectfully submit same to your attention. T

"Conditions he re are surely bad in every respect, we
haven't seen a stick of goods from the intetior, we have
experienced delays but there has never been anything quite
as bad as within the last two weeks not only can't get any
goods in but thereis no communication in the interior
whatever, and we can only hope that this is only a
temporary measure and goods will be soon coming in
otherwise we will simply have to lay our people off
until there is some improvement.
The money situation is no doubt being agitated
in the Capitals cf the world as the rovernment authorities
here have banned the ure of the National currency except
that issued by the banks in the north and as there has been
such a small issue here in Chefoo, the only bank here
operating is the Hongkong & Shanghai Bank who have been
getting their funds from Shanghai, und now these Shanghai
notes are not legal ten der and the Chinese have been
threatened with confiscation and molestation if they trade
in them, this is certainly a fine state of affairs.
The new Provisional Government have established a
note known as the Federal Bank note but it has no value in
Shanghai nor will the banks accept it for Foreign exchange,
this note is being issued here by a branch of the Yokohoma
Specie Bank, these notes can be bought with foreign exchange
at a rate of 29 whi le the national currency is being quoted
around 19 so you san see it is impossible to do any kind of
business under these conditins. Then again the Federal
notes are only good here in Chefoo and are not being
accepted in the interior. The country merchants are being
warned on the penalty of death if they trade in these new
notes.
Business here in Chefoo us ed to be a pleasure, to day
it is nothing but a headache.
If we can continue to get the northern national
currency in Ti.entsin it will be alright but if that sou rce
of supply is exhausted which is bound to be in time, it will
certainly be some problem to overcome.
These northern national currency notes when once
they get into the hands of the Provisional Government, they
are not put back into circulation and there are millions going

A telegrem of September 9, 1938, from the American
Consul at Teingtao reads substantially as follows:
At the time of the Consulate's protest to the Japa-
nese Consulate General (referred to in Tsingtao's tele-
gram of August 26, 9 a.m.) permission had been granted
to the single Tsingtao American exporter to the United
States for the ahipment of all of his then outste ding
contracts. This exporter has informed the Consulate
that Japanese competitors have recontly made sales to
firms in the United States which, unless great loss was
incurred, could only have been made at open market ex-
change rate.
The Japanese Coneul General called on Consul
Sokobin following the Consulate's written protest re-
ferred to above. The former's attitude may be summmed
up as follows: No export permits would be 1ssued to
non-Japanese exporters until they put themselves on an
oqual footing with Japanese in regard to the negotiation
of bills of exchange at official rate of exohange for
yen; at this oritical moment the Japanese could not with
equanimity observe Taingtao foreign exporters negotiating
businees at a great disadvantage over the Japanese ex-
porters beeause of the exahange situation in respect to
the official yen rate and the Chinese national curreney.
At the present moment that is the situation as far as it
ooneerms

CONFIDENTIAL
-2
concerns some suropean exporters and the single American
exporter to the United States referred to above.
Each application is considered on its own merits,
no regulations having been promulgated.
It appears to the Consulate that an exceedingly
grave development for American trade is taking place in
that the Japanese authorities are assuming to control
exohange rates and export trade. There are some curious
features of the export control, for example: (1) foreign
exporters at Chefoo have been granted permission to ex-
port without stipuletion in regard to exchange the ident-
ioal products which exporters at Tsingtao are required to
handle at fixed rate; (2) eggs are shipped by Chinese and
British companies without diffioulty. Assumption of con-
trol of imports by the Japanese authorities is a not
unlikely step whioh may be neoessary from the Japanese
point of view in order to aid the financial poliey of
the Japanese in north China by further linking imports
and exports in Chine. This step may easily follow from
the disregard of our rights in the matter of export and
exohange control.
A "yen bloe" whioh restriot(?) Japan, Manohuria and
the Tsingtao region is constantly referred to by Japamese
mewspapers. The Japanese muthorities in Tsingtao (the
Japanese spesial naval mission) will be able to achieve

Tsingtao's despatch No. 349 of August
1 amplifies a telegram (July 29, 4 p.m.)
concerning Japanese measures to compel ex-
porters to deal with the Yokohama Specie
Bank.
The despatch refers to the complete
dependence of shippers on the Japanese
Navy's permission to import and export car-
go; the exporters' refusal to heed Japanese
suggestions of the advisability of nego-
tiating their bills of exchange with the
Yokohama Specie Bank at the latter's unfa-
vorable fixed rate of exchange; Japanese
discrimination against the British banks
and in favor of the German bank; the prac-
tice of some exporters of falsely reporting
their bills as negotiated through the Gerr
bank; the sudden decision of the Japanese
Naval authorities to force exporters to
negotiate their bills with the Yokohama
Specie Bank; and the heavy resultant losses
faced by the exporters.
The despatch then discusses the serious
implications of the Japanese action: the
Japanese desire to undermine British busi-
ness interests in retaliation for British
support of Chinese currency; their ambi-
tion to tie the currency of North China
to the yen; the threatened disaster to
foreign trade; and the possible involve-
ment of the Chinese Maritime Customs.
JMJ may care to read the section be-
ginning at the middle of page 4.

The HONG KONG TELEGRAPH of September 7, 1938,
featured an article rogarding the sale in Hong Kong of
Japanese yen at a considerable discount. The article
stated that "a glut of Japanese paper currency has
appoared throughout the Far East" and that as a result
the Japanese yen "which is quoted in London at 1s. 2d.
can be purchased in Hong Kong for 8-1/2d.". The article
in question further stated that banks throughout the
Far East were refusing to deal in Japanese currency and
that even Japanese banks in Hong Kong had declined to
purchase Japanese yon. Continuing, the TELEGRAPH said:
"Actually, the Japanese money flooding the
Far East appears to be even less valuable than
the unofficial exchange rate would indicate.
The money is not negotiable in China and, appa-
rently, cannot be taken into Japan unless it is
smuggled. This is all a part of the Japanese
offort to defend her war-strained currenoy."
The TELEGRAPH advances three reasons for this flood
of Japanese currency in Hong Kong, as follows:
"One is that the new bank-notes issued by
the Japanese-sponsored Federal Reserve Bank in
North China were backed by yen and, at one period,
were quoted advantageously vis-a-vis the yen.
Japanese, Korean, and Manchukuoan speculators
immediately purchased the new bank-notes as a
speoulative buy, paying for them with Japanese
yen. The Japanese money ultimately found its way
to the southorn coastal ports, particularly Hong
Kong and Shanghai.
A second reason, the TELeGRaPH is informed,
1s that Japanese speculators sent huge quantities
of Japanese money to Shanghai, Hong Kong and other
centres in the Far East in order to purchase
foreign currencies for hoarding purposes before
the yen dropped.
The third, and one of the chief reasons, is
that until recently the Japanese forces in China,
ostimated to total over 1,000,000 men, were paid
in Japanese curreney. Naturally, they spent much
of their money in the Japanese occupied areas of
China."
A1though

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Although the foregoing press despatch is correet
in some details local bankers summarize the situation
as follows:
The Japanese government has for the past two or
three months, at least, stopped paying Japanese troops
in China in Japanese National Currency and now issues a
special fiduciary currency for the payment of its troops
in China. This currency, it is understood, is negotia-
ble, by compulsion, in the occupied areas but 1s not
negotiable in Japan.
It is possible to purchase in Hong Kong Japanese
yen at the rate of 152 yen for HK$100 by sub rosa
transactions with individuals, whereas the bank rate is
106 yen for HK$100, approximately. It is known that a
number of unofficial yen transactions have been made at
Hong Kong and that in some cases, notably those of local
Indian merchants, the money so obtained has been smuggled
into Japan and used for the purchase of merchandise. In
other cases, however, these fiduciary yen notes have been
refused acceptance as legal tender in Japan and the
purchasers have lost their entire investment in such
ourrency.
These special fiduciary notes, which resemble yen
 notes in every detail, are marked in some manner so as
to distinguish them from the yen ourrency used in Japan.
This system of marking is not generally known since even
the Hong Kong braneh of the Yokohama Specie Bank has
informed the Hong Kong & Shanghai Banking Corporation
that they are unable to guarantee the validity of yen
notes purchased from them. This question arose some days
ago when a prominemt British resident wished to buy yen
ourreney

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
currency for a visit to Japan and the best guarantee
whioh the Hong Kong & Shanghai Bank could give was
that the Yokohama Specie Bank would undertake to obtain
redress in the event that the notes which they sold to
him through the British bank were not accepted in Japan.
The Hong Kong & Shanghai Banking Corporation has
informed this office in strict confidenoe that they
were recently offered one million yen in notes by an
individual from Shanghai at a rate of approximately
152 yen for HK$100. They declined the offer and re-
ferred the vendor to the Yokohama Specie Bank which
would buy only 500 yen of these same notes.

Effeot on American Trade

According to a consular invoice certified by
this office on ugust 10, 1938, the Jupanese firm
Mitsubishi made a shipment of 286.49 tons of peanut
oil to its New York office. At the exchange rate of
28, i. e. the Jepanese official yen rate, the price
in Tsingtao after deducting frei ht, insurance and
other charges would be l3.30 per picul local currency
against the currently prevailing price of 22.00 local
cur rency per picul. There is a loss of 8.70 local
currency per picul, or about 145.00 yen per ton. On
August 13 ano ther shipment was made by the same firm
and the consular invoice certified by the American
consulate. The rice was 3.99 per 100 pounds. Con-
sidering the local price of peanuts and the prices
given in the consular invoices, the shippers (iitsubishi)
lost about 90,000 yen on the two shipments, totaling
648 tons. In addition to these two shipments to the
United States, Japanese firms have made similar ship-
men ts elsewhere.

Merchandise Balance of Trade of Japan
For the first seven months of 1938 Japanese exports
amounted to 1,412.1 million yen and imports to 1,603.7
million yen, resulting in an excess of imports of 191.6
million yen. During the corresponding period for 1937,
exports

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
expor ts were 1,810.0 million yen, 1mports 2,505.3 mi1lion
yen, and the import balance 6l8.1 million yen. Thece fig-
ures, however, give a misleading impression of the extent
of the improvement in Japan's trade balance. The ao arent
improvement arises in large part from Japan's favorable
merchandise balance vith China and "Manci.ukuo". Since the
yen is now the medium of most of the trade between these
areas and Japan, an excess of Japanese exports to these
areas cannot offset the foreign exchange deficit reculting
from an excess of Japanese imports from other countries.
If Japan's trade with Manchuria, Kwantung and North and
Central China ports is aeducted from Japan's total foreign
trade, it is found that the net import balance of Japan
for the first seven months of 1938 is increased from
the import balance for the first seven months of 1937 is
increased from 695.2 million yen to 830 million yen.
It is apparent,neverthelens,that even with this
correction the net import balance of Japen for the first
seven months of 1938 is only about half that for the
comparable period in 1937. Moreover, the Japanese Govern-
ment has undoubtedly been able to derive some foreign
exchange from the trade of Manchuria and China with the
outside world. While statistics are not available for
even
(1)
See table in Appendix I.

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.98)

even a rough guess s to the amount of foreign excnange
derived from these sources, it may be noted that for the
first six months of 1938 Manchuria's foreign trade with
countries other than Japan showed an export balance of
37.4 million yen. For the corresponding period in 1937,
the export balance of Manchuria with countries other tean
Japan was 75 million yen. As an oifset to this decline
in the export balance of Manchuria, Japan had of course
the trade of
assumed control over/a much larger area of China than in
1937.
Viewing the situation as a wnole, it would appear that
the improvement in Japan's trade balance would explain
at least in part the retardation of the rate of exhaustion
of foreign exchange and gold recources during the past
three months. Whether this improvement will continue will
doubtless depend in large part on the ability of Japan to
control the forein trade of China to its advantage, and
upon the extent to which the improved trade balance, which
reflects pximarkrx a reduction in imports rather than an
increase in exports, impairs the ability of Japan to
maintain exports in undiminished volume in the near future.
The total exports of Japan during the first seven months
of 1938 were 22 percent and imports 36 percent less tnan
during the corresponding period of 1937. This drastic
decline

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.92)

hat had such a tendency woula appear to be substantiated

8945151

894.
the sending of Shanghai drafts in the Chefoo merket, whioh is a

55

difficult position in which we are placed.
I have noted with pleasure the firm stand that

you will continue to call this to their attention in no uncertain

is no reason why an embargo should not be placed on their merchandise
coming into America - and it is my personal belief that the Chinet
government is fighting the cause of the entire world against these
totalitarian states.

Our Company, a holding company for foreign patent

with subsidiary companies, companies in which we hold less than
control and with striot licensees in which we have no direct
financial interest. Our operation in Japan, however, is through

Japanese interests. In addition to this, we have entered into
a license agreement with our Japanese subsidiary providing for
a royalty on paper bags and machines manufactured under our
patents.
Until the beginning of this year, we have always re-
ceived payment of royalty promptly, but since have been unable
to get permission for payment because of restrictions enforced
against payments abroad from Japan. However, we should state
that a dividend in 1937 was deolared and paid to us in dollars
in this year. At the present time, however, some ¥ 200,000 owing

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.98)
- 1 -
THE FISCAL POSITION OF AMERICAN COMPANIES IN
JAPAN AT THE BEGINNING OF 1939
Introduction
1938 was a period in which American companies in
Japan made every effort to protect themselves from
losses which might arise from exchange fluctuations or
from Japan's inability to obtain sufficient exchange to
meet its dollar obligations. The Finance and Commerce
Ministries, on the other hand, have utilized their
import and exchange controls to ease the pressure on
the yen and to delay as long as possible payments in
foreign currencies.
The following outline gives a brief summary of
the results of these conflicting policies as they re-
late to the credit and exchange positions in January
1939, of American companies engaged in business in the
Yokohama consular district. This report deals only
with the liquid assets of American companies in Japan
and does not undertake to discuss problems relating
to their fixed investments.
Sources of information are listed on the last two
pages of the report. The exchange rate on January ll,
1939, was: one yen equals 27.25 cents American.
PART I

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
PART I
STANDARD-VACUUN OIL COMPANY
Standard-Vacuum's oil business with Japan is
divided into two categories:
1. Petroleum products, including aviation
gasoline, sold to Japanese govornmental
agencies.
2. Petroleum products imported by the
Company directly, and sold through its
own distributional facilities for re-
gular consumer use.
Froducts in the first category are sold F.0.B.
the United States through Mitsui Bussan Kaisha.
Mitsui arranges for import and exchange permits.
credit risk by Standard-Vacuum is involved as ship-
ments are only made after receipt of irrevocable
letters of credit.
Products in the second category are imported
by Standard-Vacuum oil Company on a consignment
basis. Formerly, except when a drop in exchange was
feared, this company made no effort to purchase ex-
change to cover these imports until the products had
been sold and the proceeds remitted to the company
by its dealers. As a result of this policy Standard-
Vacuum at the end of 1937 was in the position of naving
a large potential yen balance with no permits to remit
the same to the United States. This potential yen
balance was equivalent to the combined value of
Standard-Vacuum's oil stocks, oil in transportation
in Japan, dealers' stocks, and credits outstanding
to

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
to dealers for oil previously sold. Roughly it approxi-
mates the value of the company's regular business in
Japan for a period of six to eight months.
During 1938 the company succeeded, after prolonged
negotiations, in obtaining exchange permits and forward
exchange contracts for this balance. Its credit posi-
tion at the beginning of 1939 is as follows:
1. On petroleum products sold to the govern-
ment services through Mitsui Bussan Kaisha,
Standard-Vacuum has no credit risks out-
standing.
2. On its petroleum products in Japan for
regular distribution, the company has
received permits for the future re-
mittance of exchange in sums equal,
after deductions for local expenses,
to the yen which the Japan Division
will receive for products now in the
country. The company has concluded
forward exchange contracts with the
Yokohama Specie Bank (a minor share
of the contracts are with the Mitsui
Bank) for future periodic remittances
as the yen become available from
dealers.
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company is, therefore, pro-
tected against exchange fluctuations on goods now
in Japan. Its only possible loss on these goods
would

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
would arise from the inability of the Yokohama Specie
Bank to honor its forward exchange contracts or from
intervention by the Japanese Government to prevent
the bank from meeting its obligations.
Regarding the company's future credit policy, the
Finance Ministry desires to cover Standard-Vacuum's
imports with exchange permits equal only to the C.I.F.*
value of the products and to deal separately with the
question of remitting profits. Heretofore, the company
has remitted its total yen income, after deductions.Der
local expenses. The company is attempting to retain
this basis of settlement, contending that its Japan
Division is merely a branch of an American corporation
and that it does not have a separate Japanese corporate
entity. The matter is now under negotiation between
the company and the Finance Ministry and no agreement
has been reached.
PART II
Note (*): Cost - Insurance - Freight.

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
PART II
B. F. GOODRICH COMPANY
This corporation has a small stock interest in
Yokohama Rubber Company and a contract under which it
furnishes, for an annual fee, patents and technical
asaistance for the production of tires and industrial
equipment.
Remittance of the annual stock dividend is due
in February, 1939, and negotiations with the Finance
Ministry are at present being made by the Yokohama
Rubber Company in an effort to obtain permission to
remit the necessary exchange.
The Goodrich Company has been unable to obtain
an exchange permit for the remittance of the fee
currently payable for patents and technical assis-
tance. The company has agreed to accept $21,000.00
in payments in kind and the Finance Ministry has,
after some negotiation, agreed to permit the exporta-
tion of the goods being used in lieu of dollars.
Under the plan as finally adopted, the Yokohama
Rubber Company has purchased with yen silk habutae
from S. Kamei and Company. The habutae is being
shipped by Kamei to the Goodrich plant in Akron.
Gootrich will use the tertile for the production
of raincoats processed with the new synthetic com-
pound koroseal (described in TIME, December 12, 1938,
page 64), and will credit Yokohama Rubber Company
with the payment. The first shipment under this
agreement, in the value of $ 7,575.00 was made on
December 28, 1938.
The

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.96)
一 6 -
The Janenese tovernment tous not ordinnrily permit
exportetion on this basis, but approved the barter
arrangement because the silk habutae is produced entirely
within Japan and, presumably, because the silk is being
used for a new purpose and will not enter into competi-
tion with other Japanese silk exports.
The Yokohama Rubber Company negotiated throughout
l938 with D. F. Goodrich Company in an effort to obtain
carbon black on credit. These efforts were unnuccessful
as Goodrich did not care to increase its "frozen" assets
in Japan.

FORD MOTOR COMPANY
The company operates an assembly and distribution
organization through a subsidiary, The Ford Motor
Company of Japan, Limited. In the eleven months'
period, Februery to December 1938, this company receiv-
ed exchange permits totaling $5,100,000. This sum re-
presented payments for materiels which hed been and
were being made under import permits.
Application to remit the July 1938 dividend,
amounting to Yen 2,800,000, was filed in August and
has since been a matter of negotiation between the
company and the Finance Ministry. The Finance Ministry
has given as a reason for withholding the permit, its
desire to adjust payments to the home office which Ford
has allegedly made in excess of the real C.I.F. value
of the imported automobile parts. Ford has admitted
the allegation but claims that the overvaluation has
been in an amount only large enough to cover interest
charges on the goods between the date of shipment and
the date when dollar exchange in payment is finally
received in the United States.
This time "lag" is another matter of concern to
Ford officials. The Finance Ministry for the last year
has progressively lengthened the period of time which
must elapse between the date of importation and the
date when exchange remittances will be permitted.
Exchange permits granted in conjunction with Ford's
import quota for the first quarter of the calendar

PART V
AMERICAN FIRMS SELLING DIRECTLY TO
JAPANESE COMPANIES
During 1938 there was an increasin disposition
for American firms wnich did not have established
business interests in Japan to export only against
irrevocable letters of credit. This tendency was
apparent in scrap iron, steel, industrial equipment,
airplanes and automotive equioment* lines. It was
also the ruling credit practice for a large number of
items imported for the ouroose or processing and re-
export. One exception to this reneral rule has been
the practice of American cotton exporters in granting
short-term credits on raw cotton shipments to Japan.
According to one banking source, American cotton is
coming into Japan on 120-150 days credit. This enables
the Japanese manufacturer, in many cases, to process
the cotton into textiles, export the textiles and
collect payments in foreign exchange in time to meet
this American exchange obligation. This practice is
not a new development but, rather, a continuation of
the credit basis in effect prior to the beginning of
hostilities.
One exception has come to the attention of the
reporting officer to the general rule that armament
imports have been covered by letters of credit.
Amalgamated Minerals, Incorporated, a Manila concern
with American and Filipino capital has sold to Japan
since the beginning of 1937 Yen 1,500,000.00 of
manganese ore. The Japenese importing company
(Pacific

Because of the shortage of Japanese bottoms, the
manganese has recently been shipped in Filipino

The Finance ana Comnerce Industries throughout

in normal circumstances would have been considered re-
asonable. There is no evidence available to the re-
porting orficer which indicates any relaxation in the
credit policy of American firms not having established
property and other interests in Japan.

PART VI
REMITTANCE OF PERSONAL HOLDINGS OF
AMERICANS RETIRING FROM BUSINESS IN JAFAN
During the last year a number of Americans resident
in Yokohama and planning to return to the United States
for permanent residence have been faced witn the oroblem
of obtaining permission to convert their yen holuings
into dollars. Although the total amount involved was
not large, the problem assumed rather serious proportions
to the individuals concerned. In the latter half of 1938
the Finance Ministry apparently adopted the policy of
granting exchange permits on all reasonable and bona-
fide recuests.
One retired American businees man held in Jepan
and had registered with the Finance Ministry $120,000.00
in Japanese sterling bonds. Under Japanese law, interest
on these bonds was payable only in yen. The holder re-
quested permission to export the bonds but the inance
Ministry refused because interest payments on bonds
held abroad had to be met in foreign exchange. After
negotiations extending over eight months, the Finance
Ministry granted the permit on the applicant's showing
that he was leaving Japan.

INT ERNAT IONAL BUS INESD ACHIND
CORPORATION
This corporation established s subsidiary, Watson
Business Machines Japan, Limited, in June 1937, for the
purpose of developing a Japanese market for its pro-
ducts.
The parent comnany ships business machines to the
subsidiary and collects from it an initial royalty equal
to one year's rental of the machines and subsequent
rentals equal to 30 percent of the gross rentals.
The
parent company also sells tabulating cards to the sub-
sidiary. The subsidiary company installs the machines
in private business ana sovernment ofrices on a straight
rental basis and retains title to them. Tabulating cards
are sold to the customers.
Under this program the International Business
Machines Corporation has shipped to Japan Yen 500,000,00
in machines. Import permits have been granted by the
Commerce Ministry with the stipulation that the initial
royalties will not be exported from Japan.
Net rentals, after payment of overheed expenses,
now total about Yen 170,000.00. This sum, together
with Yen 30,000.00 derived from the sales of cards,
is now deposited in the Yokohama branch of the National
City Bank. The company, in spite of repeated efforts,
has never been able to obtain from the Finance Ministry
exchange permits for the remittance of any part of this
deposit.
Permits

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
-13 -
7
Permits for the remittance of proceeds from the
sale of cards which were granted in the latter part of
1937 and the first part of 1938, totaled about $35,000.00.
The last permit was granted in March 1938. No exchange
permit has ever been granted for the remittance of
rentals. The Finance Ministry has never definitely re-
fused to approve the applications of the company for
exchange permits, but on the other hand has never pro-
mised that permits would be granted in the future.
Imports of tabulating cards are no longer per-

Germany, where a comparable situation exists. The
company's future policy is to limit machine imports
to those needed for demonstration purposes in a few
key industries.
International Business Machines Corporation
apparently regards its present investment in Japan as
a gamble. The manager of the subsidiary expressed its
policy as follows:
"If international developments are favorable, the
company will eventually get out its investment. If
unfavorable, its total potential loss on the basis
of the present investment is 200,000 "blocked" yen,
plus the depreciated value of Yen 500,000.00 in
machines which have been imported. This amount, in
comparison with the company's total foreign business,
is small."

PART VIII
AMERICAN MOTION PICTURE INDUSTRY
In September l937, the Japanese Government refused
to permit further imports of American motion pictures.
The Finance Ministry, at the same time, discontinued the
granting of exchange permits for the remittance of film
distribution income. By the middle of l938 American
motion picture companies had accumulated several million
yen, representing their proceeds from the distribution
of films in Japan. The companies, through their associ-
ation in Tokyo, negotiated with the Finance and Commerce
Ministries in an effort to obtain -
1 - exchange permits to remit their "blocked"
yen,
2 - permits to import new pictures.
In August 1938, a definite understanding was
reached with the Japanese Government. Under this agree-
ment "blocked" yen in the total sum of Yen 3,000,000
have been remitted to the San Francisco branch of the
Yokohama Specie Bank. The bank has issued to the
American companies deposit slips, agreeing to pay in
dollars at the end of a three year period the sums
deposited. These deposits are non-negotiable, non-
interest bearing, and cannot be drawn on by the film
companies until the end of the three year period.
This remittance plan had been proposed by the film
companies after efforts to obtain regular exchange permits
had failed to make progress. One film representative
reported that the association's strongest talking point
in the negotiationswas that the military services would
have

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
have the use of the dollars to finence munitions imoorts
during the next three years.
In August a definite agreement was also made to
permit the importation of a set quota of new films. The
quota was established on the basis of actual film cost.
This totaled $30,000.00 on the basis of 1 cents per
film foot.
In accordance with the agreement the comoanies shipped
the films to Yokohame but could not obtain permits to
clear them through customs. In November, the associa-
tion sent a letter (described by one representative as
an "ultimatum") to the Government, referrin& to the non-
issuance of import permits. The letter, a copy of which
the reporting officer has read, stated that the interested
companies produced and distributed news rilms throughout
the world and that these films were a powerful factor in
moulding public opinion. Reference was made to the fact
that news reels had worked adversely to Jepan vis-a-vis
the present "Incident".
The letter indicated that the
association would not be responsible for news reels pro-
ductions distributed from Hollywood, if American dis-
tributing companies were to have no future interests
in Japan.
This "ultimatum" had the desired effect and a
number of the films were released from the Yokohama
branch of the Imperial Customs a few days after the
receipt of the letter. This import arrangement carried
with it no promise by the Finance Ministry, implied or
otherwise, to permit the remittance of the proceeds from
the

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.98)
- 16 -
the distribution of those films and the American
companies are once again faced with the problem of
"blocked" yen. One film representative reported that
Yen 2,500,000.00 had already been accumulated in
addition to the amount covered by the agreement and that
l939 operations would probably result in net proceeds of
an additional Yen 6,000,000.00.
