PART X

=== Page 325 === (confidence 1.00)
-19-
PART X
CONCLUSIONS
1.
Policies of the Finance and Commerce
Ministries
Japan's major exchange objectives at uhe beginning
of l938 were to facilitate military imports and, at the
same time. to protect its remaining specie reserve of
Yen 801,000,000.
Subsequent events indicate that metnods adopted
by the Finance and Commerce Ministries to reach these
objectives were:
1.
Utilization of stocks of raw
materials previously imported
into Japan.
2.
Elimination of nonessential
imports.
3.
Approval or import permits by
the Commerce Ministry on the
bases of the Cabinet Planning
Board's estimate of minimum
needs, and the Finance Ministry's
estimate of future available
foreign exchange.
4.
Postponement, whenever possible,
of affirmative action on appli-
cations for exchange permits.
5.
Insertion of stipulations in
exchange permits postponing
actual exchange remittances until
a later designated date.
6.
Limitation of exchange permits
to the C.I.F. value of the im-
ported goods.
This program failed to protect Japan's specie re-
serve, but the failure was due primarily to the drastic
fall in its export trade, rather than to any inadequacy
of the policies of the Finance and Commerce Ministries.
The first two methods adopted by the Ministries are
outside

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
outside the scope of this report. The last four call
for comment because they are largely responsible for the
present exchange position of American firms in Japan.
a. Control over import permits.
Exchange control laws in Japan were in effect prior
to the enactment of adequate import control laws. During
the early part of 1937 large speculative imports were
made, principally by leading Japanese firms who used
their oredit facilities abroad to facilitate imports,
and who later brought extreme pressure on the Finance
Ministry to obtain exchange permits to pay for them.
It soon became apparent that a continuation of these
trends would exhaust Japan's gold supply, or would pre-
cipitate a credit crisis similar to that which occurred
in Germany in 1931-32.
Durine 1938 the Government coordinated the import
and exchange functions of dirferent departments and at
the present time imports of nonessential products are
generally prohibited and importation of essential items
is restricted within the estimates of the Planning Board
and Finance Ministry.
b. Postponement of affirmative action
on applications for exchange permits.
Officers of American companies have complained bit-
terly of their inability to obtain definite decisions from
departmental officials on their import programs for es-
sential items. The constant postponement of action on
plans of American companies having fixed investments in
Japan, has in several cases resulted in companies making
shipments to Japan which were not adequately protected by
exchange permits. This situation placed the Finance
Ministry in the advantagoous position of having the goods
within

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
-21 -
within the country ane rorced the meriean  irms to ac-
cept dererred payment arrangements.
c. Postponement
of actual exchange remittance
until latest possible date after issuance
of exchange permit.
The most important single instrument, other than
gold shipments, utilized by Japan to finance armament
imports, has been the rorward exchange program or the
Finance Ministry and Japenese banks.
As has been noted in previous sections of the re-
port, exchange permits in  large number of cases have
been granted with the stioulation that actual remittances
be made six months to a year after the actual date of im-
portation. One local banking official recently estimated
that "if the Janenese banks were forced today to settle
all forward exchange contracts, it would require the
shipment of Japan's remaining specie reserve" (Yen
501,O00,000). This estimate is evidently based on the
belief that agreements by the banks to sell dollers at
future dates exceed in value their agreements to sell
yen, by the amount indicated.
The reporting officer is unable to verify that future
commitments are that large but it is evident that the
trend has been in that direction. A representative of an
American firm was informed this month by the Commerce
Ministry that Japan's international payments this year
for 1938 imports will be so large as to make necessary
additional reductions in imports.
This forward exchange position has postponed the
complete reckoning of Japan's unfavorable international
accounts

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.98)
-22-
accounts durins 197 1..
it ronstitute, nowever,
a potential, rather than immedi: te, danger to Japan's
economy. As long as forein firms are willing to bring
supplies into Japan in return for forward exchenge con-
tracts, no large aaditional strain on the yen will result.
Should these lirms for any reason suddenly demand letters
of credit as a requisite to the shipment or essential
goods, the effects would be more serious. Ja an would
be in the position of paying foreign currency for
necessary current imports, at the same time that it
was making remittances for past imports.
d. Limitation of exchange permits to the
C.I.F. value of the imported goods.
In May or June 1938, the Finance Ministry apparently
adopted the policy of prohibiting in principle the pur-
chase of foreign exchange for the remittance of profits
or dividends. As a companion measure the Government
departments have been scrutinizing carefully all appli-
cations for permits for goods being imoorted by American
firms on a consignment basis. Exchange permits have been
issued in amounts only large enough to cover actual C.I.F.
values and the companies have been told to discuss with
a separate department of the Finance Ministry, the
question of remitting profits or dividends.
To date, little progress has been made by American
companies which have profits or dividends available for
remittance to their home offices. A manager of one of
the larger American companies in Japan has recently made
a comprehensive survey of the question of remitting pro-
fits and dividends. As far as he could learn, the
Finance Ministry has approved since last May only two
applications of this type.
At

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
- 23 -
At the same time, the inance vinistry aoparently
placed an embargo on royalty remittances. The only
case in this consular district of a royalty permit being
granted since May, involved remittances for patents sold
since the beginning of hostilities under a contract
guaranteeing payments in dollars.
II.
Future Trends
Officers of American companies not havins fixed
investments in Japan have indicated that, until such
time as conditions in the rar East become stabilized,
they will continue to ship goods to Jepan only on
letter of credit, or on short-term credits.
The problem facing American companies which have
fixed investments in Japan is much more complex. If
they are to maintain their organizations in Japan and
protect their capital investments, they must continue
to import. One large American organization* is liqui-
dating its business because it "cannot foresee the time
when Japan's exchange position will be strong enough
to permit payments abroad for its imports."
This firm deals in products which are neither
necessary for armaments nor for the export industries.
Other American firms** deal in products which are still
essential to Japan's economy, and are, therefore, in
a more favorable bargaining position. These corporations
intend to insist, as a requisite to shipment, on the
receipt
Note (*): Singer Sewing Machine Company.
Note (**): Notably Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and
Ford Motor Company.

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
receipt of permission to purchase forward exchange in
amounts oqual to the C.I.F. value of their imports.
They are willing to take the risk on any future repudia-
tion of contracts, but not on any possible devaluation
of the yen.
The prospects for remittance of profits, dividends,
and royalties are not regarded as favorable. The
Finance Ministry has made no commitments to approve
pending or future applications and firms which have
obtained or hope to obtain payments in goods have
accepted this plan because they do not believe that
permission to make dollar remittances will be granted.
The Finance Ministry's policy on remittances in the
form of goods appears to be that this method is accept-
able only when it will not result in the displacement
abroad of other Japanese exports.
The one basis for optimism concerning remittances
for profits, dividends, and royalties is thet in the
past the Finance Ministry has occasionally deviated from
its general exchange policies. The most rational ex-
planation of cases in which the Finance Ministry has
permitted remittances for items not considered as
essential, is that Japan is attempting to preserve a
good credit reputation. This is also a possible ex-
planation of the reason why government officials, in-
stead of rejecting exchange permit applications in
"hopeless cases", have merely informed the applying
firm that decision has been postponed.

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 6, 1939.
In my opinion the blocking in Japan
of the yen revenues of an American steam-
ship company is a different matter from
that of the blocking of the funds of an
ordinary American company engaged in
business with or in Japan. Steamship
lines clearly have a character more pub-
lic than that of manufacturing corpora-
tions, for example, and particularly so
when they are subsidized by the govern-
ment. It seems clear to me that if
foreign countries were allowed to block
local revenues of an American steamship
line they would have in their power to
impose a competitive disadvantage upon
American lines, a competitive disadvantage
which conceivably could go to the extent
of forcing the American company out of
business.
Germany attempted last year to block
the revenues of the United States Lines
and the Department took prompt and suc-
cessful action in the matter. The impor-
tant part of the instruction to Tokyo,
namely the part to be related to the
Japanese Foreign Office, follows almost
exactly the instruction sent to the
Embassy in Berlin.

July 12, 1939

The Honorable Cordell Hull,
Secretary of State,
Washington, D.C.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
On June 22, 1939 we asked the Maritime Commission to place be-
fore you the seriousness of our difficulties in securing United States dollars
in exohange for the yen received in Japan from our normal operations. Since
that time our concern in the matter has increased still more. e now wish,
with the knowledge and consent of the Maritime Commission, to desoribe the
situation more fully, and to ask earnestly for your vigorous intervention in
our behalf with the Japanese Government. This alone may save us at least
tomporary loss of funds, and perhaps may prevent what will amount to confis-
cation by the Japanese Government of much of those funds.
THE DEVELOPMENT OF OUR DIFFICULTIES OVER YEN
Almost immediately after the commenoement of the China
"incidont", it became diffioult for Japan to obtain all the foreign exchange
neodod for goods desired from other countries. To control the demand for for
eign exchange the Japanese Government, among other movea, required us to sub-
mit our estimates for dollar exchange six months in advance. Such estimates
were made the basis of a blanket permit if appearing reasonable to the Japan-
ese authorities. We did in fact obtain permits in agreement with our estimates
up to Maroh 1938.
Without notice on March 30, 1938 our permits were restricted to

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
peretary of State
Page No. 2
three months in advance, and were reduced to an arbitrary 50% of the amount
applied for. In May 1938 blanket per its were abolished; thenceforth applica-
tions for permits were received after the close of each month for an amount
not in excess of net actual receipts d the month, proof of having the funds in
hand being required.
Through and including the month of December 1938, permits were
being granted by the fifteenth of the month following receipt of yen, if appli-
cation was promptly made, and American President Lines suffered only an average
thirty-day detention of its funds in Japan.
Since December 1938 our permits have forced us to spread our
purohases of dollars with yen over several succeeding months. The attached
tatement (Schedule A) reflects just what that delay has meant to us.
The significance of the Japanese permit system is made more
cloar by a full appreciation of the facts that the Japanese Government is striv-
ing to keep the costs of living, and the costs of imports, from rising in terme
of yen; that consequently the Japanese Govern ent is providing suoh dollar ex-
change as it makes available at a rate of approximately U.S. 27-1/4& per yen,
and that in the principal free currency market of the Orient (Shanghai, which is
outside Japan), the yen will bring only about U.S. 12-1/2d. The value of the
yen in Japan has been maintained within a moderate variation from 1ts present
value for the last five years.
OUR PRESENT YEN SITUATION
You will note from Sohedule A that our funds blocked in Japan
were as follows at July 7, 1939:

THE CAUSES OF ASKING YOUR ASISTANCE
We ask your urgent assistance in tho matter for the following
reasons:
1. We are finanoially weakened by the lack of the funds
blooked.
2.
We are subjoot to a crippling blow in case of loss in
the conversion of the yen into dollars.
3.
We are being disoriminated against in comparison with
Japanese shipping.
4.
We are being discriminated against in comparison with non-
Japanese competitors.

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.99)
ooretary of State
Page No. 4
1. We are financially weakened by the lack of funds blocked.
On May 31, 1939, the latest date for which financial statements of our Company
are available, we had current liabilities of $3,545,826.44 and total cash bal-
ances of $454,476.10 excluding custodian funds and funds blocked in Japan and
Italy. Perhaps no more need be said to show how reat is the task of rebuild-
ing financial health in the Company, and how serious a peril it is to lose by
blocking in Japan 200, 000 of the Company's working capital.
2. We are subject to a crippling blow in case of loss in the
conversion of yen into dollars. Our required payments on long-term debt for
the twelve months commenoing April 1, 1939 are $1,071,855.66. Our estimate of
earnings for the same period, before depreciation and without any allowance
either for the purohase of new equipment or for contingencies, 1s $991,225.
Only by the most careful husbanding of our liquid capital can our debt payments
be met. The loss of $100,000 to $200,000 by the breakdown of yen value can
easily mean the difference between financial success and debt default. The
continuous sale of yen in Shanghai, the chief money market of the Orient, at
45% of their nominal value in Japan, is proof of the danger in which the yen
rests. The reports that a large part of this jettisoning of yen is on the part
of Japanese subjects is still more disturbing.
3. We are being disoriminated against in comparison with Jap-
anose shipping. Japanese lines are being permitted daily to do what they will
with United States funds obtained from their steamship operations. They are
neither subject to a system of foroed loans to the United States Government,
nor are they compelled to hold United States currency at any time they may
fear its devaluation. By contrast, American President Lines is making a forced
loan to the Japanese Government, and is compelled to hold hundreds of thousands

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.99)
tary of State
Pago No. 5
of yen of precarious value.
4. We are being disoriminated against in comparison with non-
Japanese compotitors. Under date of July 1, 1939 the following radiogram was
received from Mr. Oscar G. Steen, our vice president in the Orient with offices
at Shanghai:
FROM RELLABLE SOURCE WE HAVE BEEN CONFIDENTIALLY INFORMED
AT LEAST ONE FOREIGN LINE IS RECEIV ING PERMIT MUCH SOONER THAN
WE ARE AND FOR ENTIRE LUMP SUM REMITTANCES IN MONTH ISSUED IN-
STEAD OF FOUR MONTHS SPREAD REMITTANCES. SUGGEST U.S. MARITIME
COMMISSION ASK DEPARTMENT OF STATE PROTEST THIS DISCRIMINATION
AND PRESS IMMEDLATE ACTION ON APRIL, MAY APPLICATIONS.
We do not yot have further information as to the name of the non-Japanese steam-
ship line known to have received such preferential treatment, but we feel sure
that 1t can be ascertained by your and our representatives in Japan.
THE CONDUCT OF AMERICAN IRESIDENT LINES' BUSINESS IN JAPAN
American President Lines receives yen in Japan from three sources
only: (1) the sale of passenger tickets in Japan to residents of Japan, (2) the
shipment of freight to Japan on a oolleot basis, and (3) the shipment of freight
from Japan on a prepaid basis. Out of suoh reoeipts, the Company pays its
local expenses incurred in yen. The remainder becomes the "net receipts" refer-
red to elsewhere in this letter. These "net receipts" are the subjeot of appli-
cation to the Japanese Government, in order that United States surrency may be
purohased with them and the other expenses of ship operation paid from such cur-
ronoy.
Amerioan President Lines has consistently filed its applioations
for exchange permits in Japan immediately at the close of the several months,
and has sorupulously followed all regulations and requests of the Japanese Govern-
mont with respeot to such applications. No portion of our difficulty can be
traced to the form or substance of our applications, except that the change of

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.99)
rotary of State
Page No. 6
our corporate name was used by the Japanese Government as the excuse for delay-
ing one of our permits, which permit subsequently was issued.
Amerioan President Lines is engaged in no husiness in Japan ex-
cept the steamship business and has at no time esked for permits for dollar ex-
change except to cover the ourrent net receipts in yen from suoh business. The
Company exerts every effort to keep its net receipts in yen at the lowest possi-
ble point by booking preferentially passengers and cargo giving rise to revenues
in other currencies. The Company has for a long time refused to reserve space
at Kobe for persons eastbound who planned to commence passage at other Oriental
ports snd might bring cheap yen into Kobe with which to pay passage from there
eactward. In fact, the Company will not sell tickets in Japan to any person
who does not furnish proof of residenee there.
Efforto at cooperation wi th the Japanese Government extend on
our part even to passengers from north China who may proceed to Japan by connect-
ing carriers and meet our ships there. At last reports Japanese steanship com-
panios themselves were facilitating the transfer of yen from north China to
Japan for the purpose of purohasing for such persons transportation eastbound
from Japan. American President Lines on the other hand will not sell, in ex-
change for yen, transportation eastbound from Japan to persons departing from
north China.
In brief, American President Lines has metioulously conduoted
its Japanese business in accordance with the letter and the spirit of the Japan-
ese Government's regulations.
THE COMPLAINTS OF THE JAPANESE GOVERNMENT AGAINST US
Despite the conduct of our Japanese business in the manner just
deseribed, the Japanese Government has complained to the United States Eabassy
at Tokyo:

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.98)
poretary of State
Page No。 7
That we have discontinued paying advanoes to crew members
in yen in Japanese ports and have initiated the practice
of paying our crews in American currency outside of Japan.
And that the personnel of our steamers have been purchas-
ing with Amerioan currenoy Japanese yen at a low rate of
oxchange at Shanghai and other ports of call and are using
this currenoy for their expenses while in Japan.
That we are contracting in Japan in Japanese currency for
the carriage of freight and passengers between two foreign
ports, as for instanoe, between Shanghai and Hongkong,
causing abnormal inoreases in the amounts of our remittances.
As to the second portion of their complaint, there is not one
1ota of truth. We are so eager not to get yen, except in Japan where we cannot
do business and demend any other currency, that there is no possibility of be-
coming justly subject to that criticism, whether the practice be lawful or un-
lawful.
It is true that we have discontinued payment of orew members in
yen, and are making advances against wages in Uni ted States currency. Crew
members bitterly complained that they could never get anything baok for unused
yen. Their union leaders demanded that advances be made in United States currency
o that crew members might purohase from time to time such yen as they required,
and not be forced to draw their entire advances in yen which were for all pract-
ioal purposes valueless if not then spent.
The Department of State is probably aware of the formidable diffi-
oulties of shipowners with the off-shore labor unions on the Pacific Coast. The
management of American President Lines realized the validity of the complaints
about advances in yen. In an offort to reduce the number of disputes and strikes
taking place with maritime labor, the management agreed for the present to make
wage advances in United States currency where such ourrency is available.
The making of wage advanoes is so handled as to facilitate as
little as possible the purohase of cheap yen at Shanghai for subsequent carriage

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.99)
of State
No. 8
into Japan. Each master and each purser of our vessels is positively ordered
to make no crew advances in United States currency for the ports of Yokohama
and Kobe exoept (a) within 24-hours of arrival at the respective port, and
(b) under no ciroumstanoes until after sailing from the last preceding port.
New instructions to that effect have just been sent by radio to each of the
Company's vessels in order that there may be no misunderstanding on this sub-
ject. A copy of the instructions is attached as Exhibit 1. In addition, the
Company has warned all crew members and all members of its Oriental staff not
to traffio in cr speculate with Japanese currency. That such efforts and such
warnings are entirely effeotive, no one could give assurance. As long as the
value of the yen is left by the Japanese at about l2 at Shanghai, and main-
tained at 27d in Japan, oupidity will induce persons of every nationality to
attempt to reap the profit of carrying cheap yen into Japan. le do not feel
justified, in order to please the Japanese Government, in trying to foroe yen
advanoes on the members of our crews, and in withholding dollar advances from
them.
We, therefore, are unable to concede good faith to the Japanese
Government in refusing us foreign exohange permits for the legitimate net
receipts of our steamship business in yen in Japan, and believe that they are
either misinformed as to our intents and practices or are using the alleged de-
linquenoies on our part merely as an excuse for diverting suoh foreign exchange
as they hold to other purposes.
CONCLUSION
This letter might be summarized in a few sentenoes:
The diffioulties and delays of securing dollars for the yen proceeds
of our steamship business in Japan have steadily increased, and have
reached grave proportions viewed in the light of this Company's
finanoial affairs.
The Japanese Government in refusing foreign exohange permits to us

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.99)
retary of State
Page No. 9
is disoriminating against both non-Japanese lines in goneral and
against American President Lines in particular.
American President Lines, except as to crew advanoes, has steadi-
ly observed the extravagant requirements of the Japanese Govorn-
ment in the conduct of its Japanese fiscal affairs and in its
applioations for dollar exchange. As to orew advances, Japan's
own treatment of yen makes it impossible for us to foroe them on
crew members without causing labor trouble.
Japan's foreign exchange difficulties are driving Japan to with-
hold foreign exchange in all cases, regardless of merit, where
Japan does not get direct benefit therefrom or where sufficient
influence cannot be wielded in behalf of the applicant.
I earnestly request you to make such representations to the
Government of Japan as will induce the latter to grant permits for conversion
of our April, May, and June receipts, and thereafter grant permits monthly as
soon as there shell have elapsed a reasonable time for the scrutiny of our
respective applications.
I am not sufficiently familiar with the existing treaties between
our Government and the Government of Japan to suggest any specific action on the
part of our Government, in the nature of retaliation or otherwise, which will
afford us an effeotive remedy. I bring this matter to your attention with the
hope that you will investigate it, and with the hope also that there may be found
some means by which we may secure immunity from the annoyances to which we have
long been sub jeeted, and whieh will adequately protect our obvious rights.
Cordially yours,
W.G. McAdoo
Enols.

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 17, 1939.
In a letter of date July 12, 1939, Mr.
W. G. MoAdoo, Chairman of the Board of the
American President Lines, desoribes in detail
the diffioulties accruing to the American
President Lines as a result of the blocking
of the company's yen revenues in Japan. Since
December l938 there has been a steady increase
in the amount of the company's revenues blocked
in Japan. As of July 7, 1939, the company
had yen 720,732 so blocked. The company now
states that it is financially weakened by the
lack of funds blocked in Japan and that should
the yen be depreciated the company would be
subjected to a "crippling blow". Figures are
cited showing the financial position of the
company and the importance of the blocked
funds to the company's working capital. It is
pointed out that the loss of this amount can
easily mean the difference between financial
success and debt default.
The company also points out pertinently
that the blocking of the company's funds in
Japan is a discrimination as compared with the
treatment of Japanese shipping. Japanese
lines are permitted to do what they will with
the Amerioan funds obtained from their steam-
j
ship operations. Further, the company believes
that it is being disoriminated against as com-
pared with the treatment given at least one
foreign line which is believed to receive re-
mittanoe permits muoh sooner than the American
President

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
President Lines. The American President Lines
states that it has scrupulously observed all
the Japanese regulations but points out that
the Japanese Government has two complaints
against it. One is that the company pays its
crew members in United States currency outside
Japan and that the crew members are able to
purohase Japanese yen at Shanghai at a muoh
lower rate of exchange. The other complaint
is a charge that the company contracts in Japan
in Japanose currenoy for the carriage of
freight and passengers between two foreign
ports. The latter charge the American Presi-
dent Lines flatly denies (and its denial seems
wholly reasonable) and the first complaint it
justifies by stating that crew members bitterly
complain if they are paid in Japanese yen and
their union leaders demand that they be paid
in United States currency; and the company
does not dare engage the unions on this point.
(It seems wholly reasonable that crew members
should be paid in United States currency if
they so desire; the United States fleet sta-
tioned abroad is uniformly paid in United
States currency).
Comment: It appears that in exercising
the prerogative of withholding the yen revenues
of the Amerioan President Lines the Japanese
Government has within its power to reduce the
competition of the company or even to break it
finanoially. It would seem that this is an
eventuality whioh this Government cannot allow
to take place and that means must be found to
obligo

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
oblige the Japanese Government to institute
some effective system for the regular transfer
of the yen revenues of American companies. A
survey is being made of the weapons at hand
but they do not appear to be very adequate
inasmuch as our commercial treaty is quite old
and does not cover the contingenoy or funds
blocked by exchange control. A first step,
however, would appear to be to emphasize strong.
ly the importance which we attach to this
matter and there is attached a draft telegram
directed to this end.

The Honorable Cordell Hull
Secretary of Statep
Washington, D.C.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
Department of St
On June 22, 1939 we asked the Maritime Commission to place be-
fore you the seriousness of our difficulties in securing United States dollars
in exchange for the yen received in Japan from our normal operations. Since
894.515
that time our concern in the matter has increased still more. We now wish,
with the knowledge and consent of the Maritime Commission, to describe the
situation more fully, and to ask earnestly for your vigorous intervention in
our behalf with the Japanese Government. This alone may save us at least
temporary loss of funds, and perhaps may prevent what will amount to confis-
3
6
cation by the Japanese Government of much of those funds.
THE DEVELOPMENT OF OUR DIFFICULTIES OVER YEN
Almost immediately after the commencement of the China
"incident", it became difficult for Japan to obtain all the foreign exchange
needed for goods desired from other countries. To control the demand for for
C
eign exchange the Japanese Government, among other moves, required us to sub-
mit our estimates for dollar exchange six months in advance. Such estimates
5
FC
were made the basis of a blanket permit if appearing reasonable to the Japan-
ese authorities. We did in fact obtain permits in agreement with our estimates
F/1
up to March 1938.
FG
Without notice on March 30, 1938 our permits were restricted to

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
cretary of State
Page No. 2
three months in advance, and were reduced to an arbitrary 50% of the amount
applied for. In May 1938 blanket permits were abolished; thenceforth applica-
tions for permits were received after the close of each month for an amount
not in excess of net actual receipts f the month, proof of having the funds in
hand being required.
Through and including the month of December 1938, permits were
being granted by the fifteenth of the month following receipt of yen, if appli-
cation was promptly made, and American President Lines suffered only an average
thirty-day detention of its funds in Japan.
Since December 1938 our permits have forced us to spread our
purchases of dollars with yen over several succeeding months. The attached
statement (Schedule A) reflects just what that delay has meant to us.
The significance of the Japanese permit system is made more
clear by a full appreciation of the facts that the Japanese Government is striv-
ing to keep the costs of living, and the costs of imports, from rising in terms
of yen; that consequently the Japanese Govern ent is providing such dollar ex-
change as it makes available at a rate of approximately U.S. 27-1/4d per yen,
and that in the principal free currency market of the Orient (Shanghai, which is
outside Japan), the yen will bring only about U.S. l2-1/2c. The value of the
yen in Japan has been maintained within a moderate variation from its present
value for the last five years.
OUR PRESENT YEN SITUATION
You will note from Schedule A that our funds blocked in Japan
were as follows at July 7, 1939:

THE CAUSES OF ASKING YOUR ASSISTANCE
We ask your urgent assistance in the matter for the following
reasons:
1. We are financially weakened by the lack of the funds
blocked.
2.
We are subject to a crippling blow in case of loss in
the conversion of the yen into dollars.
3.
We are being discriminated against in comparison with
Japanese shipping.
4.
We are being discriminated against in comparison with non-
Japanese competitors.

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.98)
ecretary of State
Page No. 4
1. We are financially weakened by the lack of funds blocked.
On May 31, 1939, the latest date for which financial statements of our Company
are available, we had current liabilities of $3,545,826.44 and total cash bal-
ances of $454,476.10 excluding custodian funds and funds blocked in Japan and
Italy. Perhaps no more need be said to show how great is the task of rebuild-
ing financial health in the Company, and how serious a peril it is to lose by
blocking in Japan $200,000 of the Coripany's working capital.
2. We are subject to a crippling blow in case of loss in the
conversion of yen into dollars. Our required payments on long-term debt for
the twelve months commencing April 1, 1939 are $1,071,855.66. Our estimate of
earnings for the same period, before depreciation and without any allowance
either for the purchase of new equipment or for contingencies, is $991,225.
Only by the most careful husbanding of our liquid capital can our debt payments
be met. The loss of $100,000 to 200,000 by the breakdown of yen value can
easily mean the difference between financial success and debt default. The
continuous sale of yen in Shanghai, the chief money market of the Orient, at
45% of their nominal value in Japan, is proof of the danger in which the yen
rests. The reports that a large part of this jettisoning of yen is on the part
of Japanese subjects is still more disturbing.
3. We are being discriminated against in comparison with Jap-
2
anese shipping. Japanese lines are being permitted daily to do what they will
lau4
with United States funds obtained from their steamship operations. They are
fear its devaluation. By contrast, American President Lines is making a forced
loan to the Japanese Government, and is compelled to hold hundreds of thousands

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.99)
Secretary of State
Page No. 5
of yen of precarious value.
4. We are being discriminated against in comparison with non-
Japanese competitors. Under date of July 1, 1939 the following radiogram was
received from Mr. Oscar G. Steen, our vice president in the Orient with offices
at Shanghai:
FROM RELLABLE SOURCE WE HAVE BEEN CONFIDENTIALLY INFORMED
AT LEAST ONE FOREIGN LINE IS RECEIVING PERMIT MUCH SOONER THAN
WE ARE AND FOR ENTIRE LUMP SUM REMITTANCES IN MONTH ISSUED IN-
STEAD OF FOUR MONTHS SPREAD REMITTANCES. SUGGEST U.S. MARITIME
COMMISSION ASK DEPARTMENT OF STATE PROTEST THIS DISCRIMINATION
AND PRESS IMMEDIATE ACTION ON APRIL, MAY APPLICATIONS.
We do not yet have further information as to the name of the non-Japanese steam-
ship line known to have received such preferential treatment, but we feel sure
that it can be ascertained by your and our representatives in Japan.
THE CONDUCT OF AMERICAN PRESIDENT LINES' BUSINESS IN JAPAN
American President Lines receives yen in Japan from three sources
only: (1) the sale of passenger tickets in Japan to residents of Japan, (2) the
shipment of freight to Japan on a collect basis, and (3) the shipment of freight
from Japan on a prepaid basis. Out of such receipts, the Company pays its
local expenses incurred in yen. The remainder becomes the "net receipts" refer-
red to elsewhere in this letter. These "net receipts" are the subject of appli-
cation to the Japanese Government, in order that United States currency may be
purchased with them and the other expenses of ship operation paid from such cur-
rency.
American President Lines has consistently filed its applications
for exchange permits in Japan immediately at the close of the several months,
and has scrupulously followed all regulations and requests of the Japanese Govern-
ment with respect to such applications. No portion of our difficulty can be
traced to the form or substance of our applications, except that the change of

=== Page 404 === (confidence 0.99)
Becretary of State
Page No. 6
our corporate name was used by the Japanese Government as the excuse for delay-
ing one of our permits, which permit subsequently was issued.
American President Lines is engaged in no business in Japan ex-
cept the steamship business and has at no time asked for permits for dollar ex-
change except to cover the current net receipts in yen from such business. The
Company exerts every effort to keep its net receipts in yen at the lowest possi-
ble point by booking preferentially passengers and cargo giving rise to revenues
in other currencies. The Company has for a long time refused to reserve space
at Kobe for persons eastbound who planned to commence passage at other Oriental
ports and might bring cheap yen into Kobe with which to pay passage from there
eastward. In fact, the Company will not sell tickets in Japan to any person
who does not furnish proof of residence there.
Efforts at cooperation with the Japanese Government extend on
our part even to passengers from north China who may proceed to Japan by connect-
ing carriers and meet our ships there. At last reports Japanese steamship com-
panies themselves were facilitating the transfer of yen from north China to
Japan for the purpose of purchasing for such persons transportation eastbound
from Japan. American President Lines on the other hand will not sell, in ex-
change for yen, transportation eastbound from Japan to persons departing from
north China.
In brief, American President Lines has meticulously conducted
its Japanese business in accordance with the letter and the spirit of the Japan-
ese Government's regulations.
THE COMPLAINTS OF THE JAPANESE GOVERNMENT AGAINST US
Despite the conduct of our Japanese business in the manner just
described, the Japanese Government has complained to the United States Embassy
at Tokyo:

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
Secretary of State
Page No. 7
That we have discontinued paying advances to crew members
in yen in Japanese ports and have initiated the practice
of paying our crews in American currency outside of Japan.
And that the personnel of our steamers have been purchas-
ing with American currency Japanese yen at a low rate of
exchange at Shanghai and other ports of call and are using
this currency for their expenses while in Japan.
That we are contracting in Japan in Japanese currency for
the carriage of freight and passengers between two foreign
ports, as for instance, between Shanghai and Hongkong,
causing abnormal increases in the amounts of our remittances.
As to the second portion of their complaint, there is not one
iota of truth. We are so eager not to get yen, except in Japan where we cannot
do business and demand any other currency, that there is no possibility of be-
coming justly subject to that criticism, whether the practice be lawful or un-
1awful.
It is true that we have discontinued payment of crew members in
yen, and are making advances against wages in United States currency. Crew
members bitterly complained that they could never get anything back for unused
yen. Their union leaders demanded that advances be made in United States currency
so that crew members might purchase from time to time such yen as they reqlired,
and not be forced to draw their entire advances in yen which were for all pract-
ical purposes valueless if not then spent.
The Department of State is probably aware of the formidable diffi-
culties of shipowners with the off-shore labor unions on the Pacific Coast. The
management of American President Lines realized the validity of the complaints
about advances in yen. In an effort to reduce the number of disputes and strikes
taking place with maritime labor, the management agreed for the present to make
wage advances in United States currency where such currency is available.
The making of wage advances is so handled as to facilitate as
little as possible the purchase of cheap yen at Shanghai for subsequent carriage

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
Secretary of State
Page No. 8
into Japan. Each master and each purser of our vessels is positively ordered
to make no crew advances in United States currency for the ports of Yokohama
and Kobe except (a) within 24-hours of arrival at the respective port, and
(b) under no circumstances until after sailing from the last preceding port.
New instructions to that effect have just been sent by radio to each of the
Company's vessels in order that there may be no misunderstanding on this sub-
ject. A copy of the instructions is attached as Exhibit 1. In addition, the
Company has warned all crew members and all members of its Oriental staff not
to traffic in or speculate with Japanese currency. That such efforts and such
warnings are entirely effective, no one could give assurance. As long as the
value of the yen is left by the Japanese at about l2td at Shanghai, and main-
tained at 274 in Japan, cupidity will induce persons of every nationality to
attempt to reap the profit of carrying cheap yen into Japan. We do not feel
justified, in order to please the Japanese Government, in trying to force yen
advances on the members of our crews, and in withholding dollar advances from
them.
We, therefore, are unable to concede good faith to the Japanese
Government in refusing us foreign exchange permits for the legitimate net
receipts of our steamship business in yen in Japan, and believe that they are
either misinformed as to our intents and practices or are using the alleged de-
linquencies on our part merely as an excuse for diverting such foreign exchange
as they hold to other purposes.
CONCLUSION
This letter might be summarized in a few sentences:
The difficulties and delays of securing dollars for the yen proceeds
of our steamship business in Japan have steadily increased, and have
reached grave proportions viewed in the light of this Company's
financial affairs.
The Japanese Government in refusing foreign exchange permits to us

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.98)
Secretary of State
Page No. 9
is discriminating against both non-Japanese lines in general and
against American President Lines in particular.
American President Lines, except as to crew advances, has steadi-
ly observed the extravagant requirements of the Japanese Govern-
ment in the conduct of its Japanese fiscal affairs and in its
applications for dollar exchange. As to crew advances, Japan's
own treatment of yen makes it impossible for us to force them on
crew members without causing labor trouble.
Japan's foreign exchange difficulties are driving Japan to with-
hold foreign exchange in all cases, regardless of merit, where
Japan does not get direct benefit therefrom or where sufficient
influence cannot be wielded in behalf of the applicant.
I earnestly request you to make such representations to the
Government of Japan as will induce the latter to grant permits for conversion
of our April, May, and June receipts, and thereafter grant permits monthly as
soon as there shall have elapsed a reasonable time for the scrutiny of our
respective applications.
I am not sufficiently familiar with the existing treaties between
our Government and the Government of Japan to suggest any specific action on the
part of our Government, in the nature of retaliation or otherwise, which will
afford us an effective remedy. I bring this matter to your attention with the
hope that you will investigate it, and with the hope also that there may be found
some means by which we may secure immunity from the annoyances to which we have
long been sub jected, and which will adequately protect our obvious rights.
CordiaNy yours
Myluladoo
EncV.

SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA
July 12, 1939
ALL COMPANY OFFICES AT PORTS OF CALL OUTSIDE
THE CONTINENTAL UNITED STATES
RE: Modification of instructions to Pursers regarding
Crew Advances in Japanese and Italian Ports
Please refer to amended Comptroller's Circular dated
April 19, 1939, regarding crew advances.
Under no circumstances are U.S. currency advances authori-
zed for Yokohama, Kobe, Naples prior to day preceding call at
respective ports, and under no circumstances are such advances
authorized until after sailing from last preceding port.
Please acknowledge receipt and whether these instructions
are clear to you.
ABFook
Arthur B. Poole

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
Philippines Herald
NOVEMBER 14, 1939
YOSHIDA HAS
GOOD RECORD
New Japanese Consul, Who Is
Due Nov. 28, Has Held
Distinguished Posts
Tanichiro Yoshida, newly ap-
pointed consul general for Japan
in the Philippines, who is due
here on November 28, comes from
a similar post in Calcutta, India,
where he served for almost two
years.
A graduate of the Keio Univer-
sity, he started on the consular
service as vice-consul in the Unit-
ed States from 1920 to 1925, after
passing the higher civil service
examination for diplomatic and
consular work. .While in Ameri-
ca, he was stationed in Portland,
Oregon, and in Chicago either as
vice-consul or consul. He was at
one time also stationed in Hono-
lulu.
From 1925 to 1926 he was at
the European and American bu-
reau of the foreign office. A year
later he was appointed secretary
of the Japanese legation in the
Hague.
It was in 1930 that he became
acting consul general in Hong-
kong where he stayed for two
years. In 1932 he served in the
same capacity in Kwantung.
From Kwantung he was made
chief of the 2nd and 3rd sections
of the European and Asiatic bu-
reau of the foreign office before
he salled for Calgutta as consul
general in 1937.
Outside of these designations,
Consul Yoshida was also secreta-
ry of the Japanese delegation to
the International Conference on
Reparations in 1929 and secretary
to the Japanese party to the Xth.
General Assembly of the League
of Nations.
Receptly he was promoted to
the Chokunin rank which Is equa!
to that
or

DEPARTMENT OF STE
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
41
no. 2595, September 13, 1937, transmitting reference
FILED
material in regard to Japanese control of foreign exchange,
1/
and to enclose a translation by the Embassy of the Ministry
of Finance Ordinance no. 27, promulgated on June 23, 1939,
F
A
and published in the KANPO (Official Gazette) on the same
date, which revises in several important respects Ministry
of Finance Ordinance no. 7 of 1933, the legal basis of
present-day Japanese foreign exchange control.
The

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The principal changes found in the new ordinance,
which will be enforced from July 1, 1939, are as follows:
1. The upper limit of the amount of money
which may be carried out of Japan as travelling
expenses in any form including national currency,
foreign currency, money order, letter of credit,
and travellers checks, without the special per-
mission of the Ministry of Finance is reduced to
Yen S00. Hitherto the language of the law per-
mitted Yen 1000 in cash and an additional Yen 1000
in money orders, letters of credit or travellers
checks although in actual practice in recent
months there has not been permitted the exporta-
tion for travel expenses of an amount to exceed
Yen lo00 in money of all kinds.
2. A limitation in the amount of Yen 200
has been placed on the importation of currency
of any kind by travellers coming from abroad,
saving those cases where the importation is done
at the direction of the Government.
The reduction in the amount of money which may be
taken out of Japan as travelling expenses is a step that
has been anticipated for some time as part of the general
movement to keep Japan's foreign exchange position from
further deterioration and to prevent inflationary tendencies
in the ciroulation of the yen currency in China, and,
as stated above, has been more or less in effect in
reoent months. The restriction placed on the importation
of

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
of currency is aimed apparently at eliminating a practice
which has recently become widespread of importing yen
currency purchased at a cheap rate in China and other
free exchange markets for the purpose of discharging yen
obligations in Japan incurred at the Japanese official
rate.
The development of Japan's system of foreign exchange
control as exemplified by the present revisions seems to
be following closely and logically the pattern already
made familiar from other recently imposed systems of foreign
exchange control and would appear to require no special
comment at this time.
Respectfully yours,
Tugue 1, Dooaralys
851
Eugene H. Dooman,
FAS:C
Chargé a'Affaires ad interim.

As stated above.

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
July 27, 1939.
The American President Lines, Ltd.,
has given the Department what seems to be
a rather difficult job of special pleading
or of exercise of influence.
The Department is endeavoring to use all
the influence of words of urgency and con-
cern. Just how far it should go in special
pleading is another matter.
The company has some 700,000 yen earn-
ings blocked in Japan and wants to get them
out because, as it says:
"1. We are financially weakened
by the lack of the funds blocked.
"2. We are subject to a crippling
blow in case of loss in the conversion
of the yen into dollars.
"3. We are being discriminated
against in comparison with Japanece
shipping.
"4. We are being discriminated
against in comparison with non-
Japanese competitors."
The

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
The Department has not done much in
the way of arguing the matter with the Jap-
anese authorities.
The company's points nos. 1 and 2 do not
sharply differentiate the company from Ameri-
can exporters and other companies which also
need all the money they can get and which
would be hit if the yen value should go down
sharply. We do not use these points or
point 4.
The arguments the Department has used are
the following:
"Telegram 89, April ε, 1 p.m.
"The remiesion of yen revenues
has an obviously important bearing
upon the competitive ability of the
American President Lines in the Far
Eastern trade. The Department desires
that you acquaint yourself fully with
the situation through the agent in
Japan of the American President Lines
and that you bring this matter force-
fully to the attention of the approp-
riate Japanese authorities, explaining
that the Japanese steamship lines
operating to this country are free
to remit accumuleted balances from
this country and that we expect con-
tinued reciprocal treatment for
American steamship lines."

=== Page 436 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
"Telegram 177, June 29, 6 p.m.
"... that Japanese steamship lines
operating to this country are free
to remit accumulated balances from
this country and we expect recip-
rocal treatment for American steam-
ship lines..."
The rest of our telegrams have
been merely phrases of urgency.
FE's current draft telegram, after
saying that this Government desires to be
informed of an approximate date on which
it may expect a reply from the Japanese
Government and hopes that the Embassy can
elicit some definite immediate response,
says in the guise of argument:
"The remission of operating yen
revenues has an obviously important
bearing upon the competitive ability
of American shipping companies in
the Far Eastern trade as well as
upon their financial position. The
application of exchange control in
Japan to the operating revenues of
American shipping companies competing
in

=== Page 437 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
in the trans-Pacific trade constitutes
an unequal burden upon those American
companies in relation to their Japanese
competitors, and it is evident that
fair competition in the shipping trade
is achieved only through the exercise
of reciprocity. In future conversa-
tions with the Japanese Foreign Office
in regard to this matter you may use
the foregoing argumentation in addition
to that ccntained in the previous
telegrams on this subject."
All of these telegrams are exceedingly
weak in argument, and so is the American
President Lines' letter, disregarding its
point no. 4 as the Department has dis-
regarded it. Point 4 is an allegation that
Japan is discriminating against the Lines
in comparison with other non-Japanese lines.
If that were demonstrable, this Government
would have a very strong argument. So
would it if it could successfully allege
discrimination against the Lines in compari-
son with Japan's shipping. If we can prove
"discrimination" we can simply ask as of
right that the discrimination be removed
and that the American lines be treated
as favorably as either Japanese or other
foreign lines.
Point 3 made by the Lines is as

=== Page 438 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
as follows:
"We are being discriminated
against in comparison with Japanese
shipping. Japanese lines are being
permitted daily to do what they will
with United States funds obtained
from their steamship operations. They
are neither subject to a system of
forced loans to the United States Gov-
ernment, nor are they compelled to
hold United States currency at any time
they may fear its devaluation. By
contrast, American President Lines
is making a forced loan to the Jap-
anese Government, and is compelled
to hold hundreds of thousands of yen
of precarious value."
Here the Lines omit to say who is
discriminating against them. What they
mean in fact is that the United States Gov-
ernment is treating Japanese lines better
than the Japanese Government is treating
American lines. That is hardly a basis
for charging Japan with discrimination
against American lines. All that really
is in this point 3 is an argument equally
valid for every American interest having
blocked funds in Japan or any other
country, namely that the United States
permits payments to be made freely from
this

=== Page 439 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
this country to foreign countries whereas
Americans are not permitted freely to with-
draw their funds from countries which have
exchange control. While this argument,
such as it is, is equally good for any
business concern operating in any line of
business, the Department has accepted it
as the reason for asking special treatment
of American steamship lines by Japan, this
being substantially the only reason cited
in the above quoted telegrams. The lines
want to be sure that their money gets out
of Japan promptly irrespective of what
happens to any other American money in
Japan. The Department asks Japan to give
them the treatment they reguest because of
a reason which is equally good for any
American enterprise.
The present FE draft telegram attempts
to use the contents of point 3 in the follow-
ing sentence:
"The application of exchange
control in Japan to the operating
revenues of American shipping companies
competing in the trans-Pacific trade
constitutes an unequal burden upon
those American companies in relation
to their Japanese competitors, and
it is evident that fair competition
in the shipping trade
is achieved

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
only through the exercise of reciprocity.'
Here the argument continues weak, but
the special pleading goes pretty far.' There
is no statement that Japan is imposing an un-
equal burden on American companies in rela-
tion to their Japanese competitor. That
would be a charge of diccrimination. What
is said is that the effects of Japanese ex-
change control are not as hard on Japanese
shipping companies as on American shipping
companies. To this is added a statement
that fair competition in the shipping trade
is achieved only through the exercise of
reciprocity. That would apparently mean
that Japan should apply exchange control
to shipping of countries like Germany and
Italy which practice exchange control and
should not apply exchange control to coun-
tries such as the United States, Great
Britain, and France, which do not exercise
control over excnange. That, we say, is
the only way to achieve "fair competition
in the shipping trade" and we apparently
imply that Japan has some duty to assure
"fair competition" rather than non-diecrim-
inatory treatment.
The basis on which international
shipping has lived for many years is that
each

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
each country should treat all shipping,
including its own, alike, irrespective
of the flag under which a ship may sail.
To substitute a principle that each
country must reciprocate whatever treat-
ment its shipping receives in each other
country would apparently be to throw
away the right to claim national treat-
ment as well as the less valuable right
to claim most-favored-nation treatment.
It may be that the mention of
"reciprocity" in the draft telegram is
intended to imply a threat. However, if
the United States should reciprocate by
blocking the operative earnings of Jap-
anese shipping companies in the United
States, this action would hardly satisfy
the American President Lines' request for
assistance. That steamship company fears
that the value of the Japanese yen may
break in two, causing the company a loss
of $100,000 on its funds blocked in Japan.
The same development that would cause
this loss to the American company, would
double the yen value of any funds of Jap-
anese shipping companies we might block
in the United States. This would hardly
achieve "fair competition in the shipping
trade", although it would be "reciprocity".

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
some internationally recognized ground for
grievance, rather than on any reference
tending to establish a principle of reci-
procity as to exchange control. Moreover,
if we wish to urge prompt transfer of the
net yen operating earnings of American
shipping companies while not attempting to
expedite transfer of the net yen operating
earnings of Fora or General Motors, our
argument may best rest in some distinction
between shipping and other operations. This
ought to be feasible enough.
Could we say something on the follow-
ing lines:
"Obstruction to the transfer
to the home office of earnings of a
shipping company in a foreign country
over and above its expenses there,
obviously runs counter to the uni-
versally recognized principle that
governments shall avoid flag discrim-
ination even in favor of the shipping
under their own flag, since inability
to use earned funds may impair the
competitive ability of foreign shipping
vis-à-vis national shipping. This
has been generally recognized and,
so far as concerns American shipping,
arrangements have been made by Ameri-
can

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
Moreover, the sentence under discussion,
the subject of which is "the application of
exchange control in Japan to the operating
revenues of American shipping companies"
would seen to mean that we are trying to
obtain exemption of American shipping companies
from the application of exchange control in
Japan to their operating revenues. However,
what we are asking is not exemption from the
application of exchange control. Exchange
control exists when there is a requirement
that no money be sent abroad unless a govern-
ment permit has been obtained for the in-
dividual transaction. The Lines do not mind
that. What they want is that there be prompt-
er transfer of those funds which the Jap-
anese exchange control does now allow to be
transferred, but subjects to delay. The
thing the Lines would least like would be
a full reciprocity with American exchange
practice, since the removal of all Japanese
exchange control would no doubt plunge the
yen to a low value geatly enhancing the
competitive position of the Japanese shipping
lines.
It may be that we wish to hint at retal-
iation, and that while retaliation need not
take the form of reciprocal action of the
same type as the action complained of, the
handiest and most feasible weapon would be
the blocking of Japanese shipping funds.
However, retaliation should be based on

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
-11-
can shipping companies with govern-
ments practising exchange control to
assure prompt transfer of the company's
legitimate net earnings. The American
lines now doing business in Japan find
their operations hampered and their
competitive position impaired by the
delays recently imposed by the Jap-
anese authorities on transfer of their
net earnings. The payments involved
are not large in absolute amount and
this Government expects that the Jap-
anese Government, which draws large
amounts of foreign exchange from the
earnings of its shipping transactions in
the United States will wish to so
operate its exchange control operatione
as to give practical effect to the
principles of non-discrimination and
national treatment by which interna-
tional shipping is regulated".

Mr. Goertner called with the principal object of

3

this Government has at its command in combating disabil-

F/FG

=== Page 446 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
believes is the only provision of our shipping laws which
is applicable. It will be noted that this section empowers
the "board" to investigate the action of any foreign govern-
ment with respect to the privileges afforded and burdens
imposed upon vessels of the United States and to report
thereon to the President who is empowered, in case he is
unable by diplomatic action to secure equal privileges
for vessels of the United States, to advise Congress as to
the facts and his conclusions. Mr. Goertner seems to think
that if the Japanese Government should refuse to establish
some regular system for the regular remittance of the yen
revenues of the American President Lines the next logioal
step might be to tell the Japanese Government that the
Maritime Commission will be obliged to make an investiga-
tion under Seotion 26 of the Shipping Aot of 1916 and that
suoh investigation will be given full publicity.

=== Page 447 === (confidence 0.99)
Shipping Act, 1916
Section 26
Sec. 26. The board shall have power, and it shall be its duty
whenever complaint shall be made to it, to investig te the action of
any foreign Government with respect to the privileges afforded and
burdons imposed upon vossels of the United States engaged in foreign
trade whenever it shall appear that the laws, regulations, or prac-
tices of any foreign Government operate in such a menner that vessels
of the United States are not accorded equal privileges in foreign tra e
with vessels of such foreign countries or vessels of other foreign coun-
tries, either in trade to or from the ports of such foreign country or
in respect of the passage or transportation through such foreign country of
passengers or goods intended for shipment or transportction in such vessels
of the United States, either to or from ports of such foreign country or
to or from ports of other foreign countries. It shall be the duty of the
board to report the results of its investigation to the President with
its recommendations and the President is hereby autlorized and empovered to
secure by diplomatic action equal privileges for vessels of the United St tes
engaged in such foreign trade. And if by such diplomatic action the Pres-
ident shall be unable to secure such equal privileges, then the President
shall advise Congress as to the facts and his conclus ons by speci1 mes a e,
if deemed important in the public interest, in order that proper ction
may be taken thereon.

Washington, D.C.
Dear Mr. Farbach:
In the absence of enator Mc4doo from the city, I have seen
your letter of July twenty-seventh addressed to him on the subject of our yen
accounts.
Pursuant to the suggestion c ntained in the last paragraph
of your letter, I am enclosing a copy of a letter dated July 6, 1939, just
received from Mr. 0. G. Steen, our vice president in charge of Orient. Mr.
Steen's letter provides further detail concerning the radiogram previously
quoted to you and the State Department, indicating belief that discrimination
was eing practiced by the Japanese Government in /rantin: foreign exchange
permits to steamship companies.
You will note that our Mr. MacDonald received information
concerning foreign exchange permits to a French steamship line from a Mr.
Gueguen on a confidential basis. Obviously we want you to have all informa-
tion on the subject which comes into our possession. We should like, however,
not to have the source of our information disclosed farther.
We prepere for our own use a weekly statement of our yen
position as at the close of business the preceding Friday. It may be helpful
to you, and to the State Department, to watch these weekly reports, and I am,
therefore, making arrangements for them to be sent to you, and to the State
Department, immediately upon preparation until further notice.
I should like to add to the gratitude already expressed by
the Senator and Mr. Sheehan, my own appreciation of the results already obtain-
ed by intervention in Tokyo on our behalf.
Yours very truly,
113 Loob
Arthur B. Poole
Vice President

July 6, 1939
FROM:
EXECUTIVE DEPART ENT, SHANGHAI
SUBJECT: REMITTANCE PERMITS - JAPAN
Confirm our private radio No. 76 reading:
"OUR 68 FROM RELIABLE SOURCE WF HAV: BEEN CONFIDENTIALLY INFORMED
THAT AT LEAST ONE FOREIGN LIN: IS RECEIVING PER ITS MUCH SOONER
THAN WE ARE AND FOR ENTIRE LUMPSUM REMITTANCE IN MONTH ISSUED
INSTEAD OF FOUR MONTHS SPREAD REMITTANCES SUGGEST U.S. MARITIME
COMMISSION ASK DEPARTMFNT OF STATE PROTEST THIS DISCRIMINATION
AND PRESS FOR IMMEDIATE ACTION ON APRIL/MAY APPLICATIONS"
This radio was sent on the strength of confidential advices we
received from our Mr. W. G. MacDonald contained in his road letter No. 13 of
June 24th as follows:
" The following information obtained from Mr. Gueguen is strictly
confidential. Yesterday, June 23rd, the French Ambassador to Japan
signed in Tokyo a treaty with the Japanese Government whereby
Japanese products will be allowed entry into France and French
possessions. The main interest on the part of the Jananese is to
export cotton textiles to French colonies in Africa. One of the
benefits to the French is that they are to get very prompt action
on remittance permits for funds thev wish to remit from Japan,
which I am told amount to considerable on freight business; not so
much on passenger collections. They informed me that money collected
prior to May is already remitted. That they expect to get their May
permit and accomplish the remittance before the end of June, and get
immediate action on future permits, or else ...! When Mr. Gueguen
saw that I made notes regarding this he asked that the information
not be passed on".
We have also heard that German and Italian lines are receiving favor-
able consideration. In fact, one report received was to the effect that if
remittances of German funds in Japan were delayed similar treatment would be
given Japanese funds in Germany. Our information, however, is not from as
reliable a source as in the case of the Messageries Maritimes, but we are work-
ing on this and if we get anyt ing of a reliable nature will advise you.
Very truly yours,
Signed
O.G. Steen
Vice President
in Charge of Orient

The Japanese Ministry of Foreign Affairs presents
its compliments to the American Embassy at Tokyo and
has the honor to forward herewith copies of a sumaary
of the revisicn of the Crdinance of the Ministry of
Finance relating to foreign exchange control which
was pronulgated in the Official Gazette on June 23,
1939. It is to be noted that the Denartmental
Ordinance above referreo to was revised on October 8,
1939. As a result of these revisions, it is inpera-
tive that in case either Japanese currency or foreign
currency is to be taken abroad a report must be pre-
sented to the Customs authorities.
Further, even in
cases in which either the Japanese currency of foreign
currencies are taken abroad in an amount within the
allowed limit (¥500 under the latest revision), it is
necessary to obtain a permit when either of the currencies
is to be taken abroad through the medium of Yen lo0 notes.
Tokyo, July 11, 1939.

Enclosure no. 2 to despatch

A Summary of the Revision of the De artmental Creinance
of the Ministry of Finance regarcing the Foreign
Exchange Control (Departmental Ordinance no. 27
and no. 28 of the Ministry of Finance under date
of June 23, 1939) and matters worthy of attention
in connection therewith.
(I) The followirg are inportent points which became
effective on and after July 1, 1939, as a result of the
revision of Departmental Ordinance no. 7 of the Ministry of
Finance in 1933:
1. The amount allowable as the travelling expenses
of anycne leeving Japan for foreign countries
shall be reduced to ¥5o0, currency, bank draft,
or letter of credit.
2.
Under the new regulations it shall be necessary
to obtain permission of the Minister of Finance
for the importation of bank notes of Japan in
an amount exceeding ¥ 200.
3.
Anyone who imports bank notes of either Janen
or foreign countries shall present to the customs
authorities a report concernin such importation.
(II) The following matters are worthy of attention in
connection with the enforcement of the revised Departmental
Ordinance:
1. Although anyone who formerly left Japan for a
trip abroad could carry with him travelling ex-
penses up to a total of ¥2,ooo, comprising
¥1,000 currency and ¥1,000 in the form of a
draft or a letter of credit, it is now necessary
to obtain a permit when the total amount of currency,
draft or letter of credit exceeds ¥500.
2. An application for permission concerning the
carrying of the travelling expenses shall be
presented to the Bank of Japan or its branch
office as heretofore.
3. Anyone who travels abroad carrying with him a
sum exceeding Y500 shall, as far as possible,
endeavor to purchase tickets for railroads or
ships after obtaining a permit.
4.

This refers to your D.l02 of July 14th on the above subject.
Our investiations developed the following:
The C.P.R. here advise that they are not receiving permits as rapidly as

in Yokohama.
Dodwell & Co. who handle many outside lines find that their permits are slow
1n being approved. While their Yokohama Branch have received some permits
reeontly. they state that for Kobe since last February they have received
none.
Java China & Japan Line advise that they are also in difriculties, but in a
way it is their own fault. They have sent several ships from Japan to Dairen
with cargo, and have found that while the rates were high and cargo plentiful,
the Finance Department are not particularly anxious to approve their applications
for remittances.
They are behind about three months.
Pao advise th: t their remittance permits have been held up since last February
and tht they  re now outatanding about n.500,000,00. They also have
diffioulty in getting permission to disburse funds in payment of stevedoring.
There are many reasons why they are being penalized of which you are doubtless
aware.
The States Iine have few occasions to apply for permits, but have not been
particularly embarrassed.
We have not been able to develop what the German Lines are up axainst, but under-
stand they have no particular difficulties in maxing remittances.
The Messageries Maritimes' agent advises that through various trade agreemonts,
his Company has little or no difficulty in having their applications approved.
specially after he ws authorized by is Company to threaten retaliatory measures
if their permits were delayed.
It would therefore appear th.t the foreiin linesother than
the German and the French are all in the ame boat.
We have talked to several merchants such as Dupont de Nemours
& Co. etc. and they advise that. they are in difficulties. Dupont Nemours & Co.

Dear Mr. Secretary:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Departmeht of State
Since my telegram to you of August 25, the yen
has dropped from 27t¢ to 25¢, entailing a loss on our re-
ceipts which the Japanese government has impounded for so
long a time, of $9000. Unless the Japanese government can
be induced to make restitution, I feel that we have a right
to demand this, and I hope sincerely that this point may be
stressed in your communications with our Ambassador at Tokyo.
894.5151/1
I am afraid the situation is going to grow pro-
gressively worse and that this company will be compelled
to suffer serious losses because of the Japanese govern-
ment's action.
I do not know the terms of our treaties with
Japan, but I hope that they contain provisions which protect
American citizens against the arbitrary action the Japanese
government has taken against our company up to the present
time. For instance, there must be mutual obligations to
9
accord to the vessels of each power in the ports of the
3
other, equal and fair treatment. If this be true, can we
not impound the receipts of Japanese vessels in our ports
to the extent that it may be necessary to protect us against
confiscation of our receipts by the Japanese government in
the ports of Japan?
I hope that you may be able to exert continuing
pressure on Japan until we get the money that is due us.
I regret very much to have to annoy you with
this matter, but it is a matter of supreme importance to
this company.
Cordially yours,
ng uealao

A
DEPAE
Dear Mr. Secretary:
Please let me thank you for yours of August
25th concerning our impounded revenues in Japan. Since
894.5151/164
its receipt we have been paid for our June account, and
are hoping to receive soon payment for the July account.
I quote from your letter of August 25th:
"The Department has received from the American
Embassy at Tokyo & tel gram under date August
19 to the effect that on the date indicated the
Japanese Foreign Office informally made known to
the American Embassy that hereafter there will
be issued regularly by the Japanese Ministry of
Finance permits for the transfer of the monthly
revenues of the American President Lines; that
the permits will entail a time lag of two months;
as, for example, there will be issued in Septem-
ber the permit covering the June remittance;
and that in the immediate future this arrange-
ment will, by formal note, be comnnunicated to
the American Embassy."
1. We protest earnestly against the two months'
delay in payment of our accounts as suggested by the
Japanese government. There is, as I said in my letter
to you of August 29, no reason of which we can conceive
for the arbitrary action of the Japanese government in
taking a large part of our working capital for their own
purposes. It is, in fact, a forced loan without interest.
Moreover, if we were permitted to collect our accounts in
Japan at the current rate of exchange at the time they
accrue, we would not be subjected to the risk of a decline
in the rate of exchange during the two months' lag which
F/FG
the Japanese government imposes upon the payment of our
accounts.

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.97)
2.
As stated in my letter of August 29th, there
must be in our treaties with Japan express provisions
for reciprocal treatment for the ships of each govern-
ment in the ports of the other. This provision un-
doubtedly must be broad enough not only to cover all
transactions relating to the entry and departure from
ports, but also payments to the companies operating the
ships of the charges made by them for the transportation
of passengers and cargo on these ships.
We are certainly giving to Japanese ships in
our ports the most favored nation treatment. I certainly
hope that we have in our treaties such provisions as will
enable us to impound the revenues of Japanese ships from
American cargoes in our ports until they are sufficient
to indemnify us against the amount of revenues from our
ships which are unjustly impounded by Japan. I shall be
greatly interested in your reply to this point.
With thanks for the cooperation you have con-
tinually given us in this matter, I am,
Sincerely yours,
mohiladon
Chaitman of the Board
The Honorable
The Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.98)

has stated. Having by persistent and vigorous represen-

more favorable than have recently been granted, the

the Ameriean Embassy at Tokyo reads substantially as
follows:
The Japanese Government has been seriously cen-
Gerned over the drop in the croes rate between New York
and London within the laet few days. The Government
had, following the fall in the rate fro approximately
$4.68, been giving consideration to action in prepara-
tion for a further drop. The proposed aotion whioh was
disouesed was given no publioity. Dissolving of the
link to British ourreney and pegging to the dollar had
been contemplated by the Government as a possible re-
course in the event that fluotuation in the cross rate
should exoeed a maximum range of four cents (from
$4.02 te $4.06). Now -- it has been learned by the
Embessy -- the question of the course of counter aotion

mere being censidered by the Goverament. The Eabassy

My dear Mr. Secreary:
MR. GRADY
m
894.5151/171
Permit me to thank you for your letter of September
19th. I am glad to inform you that since the date of your
letter we received remittances for our August yen account
in Japan. This would seem to indicate that the Japanese
government has acceded to the position we have heretofore
taken that our yen accounts should be settled within the
next month after their accrual. If the Japanese government
should continue to do this, it will be entirely satisfactory
to us.
We appreciate the reasons you have outlined in your
letter for not making further suggestions to the Japanese
government at this time concerning the treatment of our
vessels. If an unfavorable change should occur in the
situation, we shall take the liberty of communicating with
OCT1319

December
DIVISION OF
Divisionof
FAH EASTERN ARFAIRS
Department of State
JAN 8 - 1940
Washington, D.C.
Dopartrent of St
Gentlemen:
894.5151
Re: FE 894.5151/139
For several months the situation with respect to our conversion
of yen receipts into dollars has been in reasonably satisfactory condi-
tion. That is, a permit for each month's receipts has been received prior
to the end of the succeeding month.
The amounts of yen receipts awaiting collection have continued
to be comparatively small. Under these conditions, it does not seem neces-
sary to burden you with continued weekly reports. Unless it is your desire,
8
therefore, or the desire of the Maritime Commission, we shall discontinue
6
with the present statement the forwarding of weekly reports of our yen
position.
We shall, of course, communicate with you and with the Maritime
Commission again promptly in case the conversion of yen receipts into
dollars does not in the future continue to proceed as satisfactorily as
it does at present.
We take this opportunity to state again our gratitude for the
assistance rendered us vis-a-vis the Japanese government.
We are also sending a copy of this letter to the Maritime Com-
mission expressing to them, as well, our appreciation of their efforts
in the same matter.
Yours very truly,
A3Foole
Arthur B. Poole
Vice President
ABP:D
Encl.
cc: U.S. Maritime Commission

in the course of collection. No forward exchange contract for the
December receipts has been, or can lawfully be, entered into prior to
application for and receipt of a permit covering December receipts as
a unit.
A.B. Poole
December 27, 1939
